#3252. Potholes, bus fares and headphone dodgers: will Burnham’s strategy of ‘everyday fixes’ succeed?

New PM has prioritised small fixes to show voters Labour can make a real difference, and government insiders are delighted at the public’s reaction

In the first episode of Armando Iannucci’s political satire The Thick of It, the hapless minister Hugh Abbot is in the back of a taxi, frantically brainstorming for an on-the-hoof policy he can announce to the assembled national press.

“So you want something sexy and eye-catching, and that is free and universally popular and instantly applicable. No one can possibly object to it,” says the addled junior adviser Ollie Reeder. “Well, really, you should have said something before then, because I’ve got a file about that fucking thick of that back in the office.”

After a three-month micro-policy blitz from Downing Street, the question now being asked is: did Reeder pass said file on to the government’s battalion of special advisers? In the past week Andy Burnham has vowed to clamp down on social pariahs blaring out TikTok videos on public transport, outlaw “rip-off” private car park fines and – on Thursday – fill in potholes in 7,000 miles of England’s roads.

Before that, as part of the focus on “everyday fixes”, the prime minister announced a plan to remove VAT from electricity bills, a £2 cap on single bus fares across England and take action on “auto-renew” subscriptions. There’s also promises to crack down on pricey branded school uniforms, ban specious discounts, limit vape and betting shops and stop airlines charging families to sit together on planes. At this rate all minor annoyances in the UK will be eradicated by Christmas.

The idea is simple, says one policy adviser. “People expect the government to be able to do something about things which are unfair – big or small,” they said. “It sends a message that the government is on people’s side, its ready to act, and it can genuinely make a difference.”

Government insiders say the policies are not the product of standing around in a circle, throwing a stress ball and shouting out ideas. “There isn’t someone with a whiteboard brainstorming new bicycles for special constables,” says one, furthering the Thick of It comparison. Instead, the everyday fixes were a central policy strategy from Burnham’s first day in office. “We did this sort of thing under Keir too,” said another source. “But under Andy it’s a whole different thing; he’s put rocket boosters under it.”

In the first days of his premiership, Burnham gave his close ally Louise Haigh, the first secretary of state, the job of establishing taskforces for three core areas – cost of living, public control and regeneration. One government source said: “There is a very intentional drumbeat of those because they do make a difference, but also it’s constantly showing people that we understand where those frustrations and low-level pressures are.”

Ideas are “kicked around” between the cost of living taskforce in the Cabinet Office and the policy unit in No 10, with all departments encouraged to feed in. “There’s a sort of filtering up process,” the source said. “It’s quite a sprawling sort of cross-government approach.”

Another said that while some departments were better placed to find “not hugely spendy” fixes, every minister was encouraged to contribute. “I think a lot of people had sort of resigned any hope that the government can actually get things done – and this is showing them that, yes, actually, we can,” another source said.

The small policy announcements have been, one delighted adviser said, a roaring success. Another source said: “Some people are very snotty about it, but in a world where it’s really hard for a government to cut through, this is working. You put some ideas through the grid and you often don’t know if they’re going to fly. But when you see one go gangbusters, it’s kind of like Christmas morning.”

One single fact policy idea dominated the news cycle for an entire day. Another generated tens of thousands of posts on X. Burnham’s faintly hokey video on antisocial phone use garnered 2m views. “It helps that he’s just really good at it,” the source said.

The small-fix policies have not been appreciated by everyone. Reform UK’s Robert Jenrick said the UK was “going down the pan and our prime minister is faffing around tweaking parking tickets” – despite trying to implement the policy himself when he was a Conservative minister. The former Olympian Sharron Davies said: “As Paris burns our prime minister has stopped people playing loud music on buses & trains,” leading some to question whether she expected Burnham to invade the French capital.

Kemi Badenoch, meanwhile, has been dismissive, saying in a speech this week that cracking down on loud music on public transport was a “job for ticket inspectors, not the prime minister”. Reprising the theme at prime minister’s questions on Wednesday, the leader of the opposition implicitly accused Burnham of fiddling while Rome burns. “We are spending more on interest payments than we do on schools, the army and the police combined,” she said. “Meanwhile, the prime minister is worrying about loud music on trains. This is not serious.”

Across the dispatch box, Burnham hid his delight at her response behind a disappointed look. “I’m not going to apologise for doing the small things that help people have better lives,” he said sombrely, while his advisers likely high-fived in private.

Regardless of the reaction of Burnham’s opponents, the focus on minor grievances is a canny strategy that appears to be working, said the pollster Luke Tryl, the director of More in Common. In recent polling, when people were asked whether Burnham understood the pressure of high energy bills on ordinary people, 45% said he did. When the same question was asked about Starmer, that figure was 23%. “I think the Tories and Reform have got it dead wrong on this,” Tryl said. “People bring this sort of stuff up in focus groups all the time. It cuts across left- and rightwing voters. People care about immigration, about the NHS – but they also care about everyday frictions.”

But behind the scenes there is an acknowledgment that a focus on ticking off small things from the PM’s to-do list will be significantly more challenging in moments of crisis. There has to be a “twin-track” approach, said one insider. “It’s combining a big picture, long term, dismantling 40 years of neoliberalism and addressing questions about ownership and control, but at the same time tackling more immediate daily bugbears,” they said. “What you’ve seen so far is these smaller level interventions, but the bigger structural, longer term piece will be in the 10-year plan.”

While the everyday fixes may be popular, the sugar-high they produce could be short-lived, said Ed Shackle, the head of qualitative research at the policy consultants Public First Global. “The government should be under no illusion that as soon as any real substantive event rears its head, any small popularity boost will be instantly blown away,” he said. “Quick fixes are popular, but they are a poor substitute for fixing the big issues the public care about.”

#3253. Yemen'de hükümet güçleriyle Husiler arasındaki çatışmalar Taiz ve Lahic vilayetlerinde sürüyor

Yemen Ordu Sözcüsü Macid en-Nuzeyli, ABD merkezli X şirketinin sosyal medya platformundaki açıklamasında, hükümet güçlerinin son 24 saatte Husilerin askeri kapasitesine yönelik 753 hassas ve doğrudan saldırı gerçekleştirdiğini belirtti.

Nüzeyli, saldırılarda Husilerin ileri komuta ve kontrol merkezleri, yönetim birimleri, tahkimatları, ateş mevzileri, toplanma alanları, takviye birlikleri ile ulaşım ve ikmal araçlarının hedef alındığını kaydetti.

Saldırılarda 218 Husinin etkisiz hale getirildiğini belirten Nuzeyli, 78 askeri araç, teçhizat ve silahın imha edildiğini ya da kullanılamaz hale getirildiğini aktardı.

Yemen ordusuna bağlı Taiz Askeri Komutanlığından yapılan açıklamada, hükümet güçleri ile halk direniş birliklerinin, Taiz'in güneyindeki Misrah ilçesine bağlı Akrud cephesinde Husilerin düzenlediği saldırıyı püskürttüğü bildirildi.

Açıklamada, saldırıya katılan Husilerden ölen ve yaralananlar olduğu belirtildi.

Yemen'in nüfus bakımından en büyük vilayeti Taiz'de kontrol farklı güçler arasında bölünmüş durumda.

Hükümet güçleri Taiz kenti ile bazı bölgeleri kontrol altında tutarken, Husiler son haftalarda kırsal ve kıyı kesimlerinde bazı bölgeleri kontrol altına aldı.

Yemen'in güneybatısındaki Lahic vilayetinde hükümet güçleri yaptıkları açıklamada, Babulmendeb Boğazı'na bakan Mudaribe cephesinde Husilere ait 2 askeri aracı imha ettiklerini duyurdu.

Hükümet güçleri Lahic vilayetinin büyük bölümünü kontrol ederken, Husiler Taiz sınırındaki Mudaribe ilçesinin bazı bölgeleri ile Kubeyta ilçesinin bir bölümünde kontrolü elinde tutuyor.

Yemen Haber Ajansı SABA'nın haberine göre, Enformasyon Bakanı Muammer el-İryani ülkenin güneyindeki Şebve vilayetinde Husilere bağlı 13 kişilik bir hücre ile Husilere gönderilmek üzere silah ve patlayıcı yüklü bir kamyonun ele geçirildiğini açıkladı.

İryani, ele geçirilenler arasında yaklaşık 500 otomatik silah, keskin nişancı tüfekleri, 120 ve 106 milimetrelik mühimmat, yüksek tahrip gücüne sahip patlayıcı düzenekler ile insansız hava araçlarında (İHA) kullanılmak üzere tasarlanmış mühimmat, bombalar ve İHA parçalarının bulunduğunu kaydetti.

Yemenli Bakan, operasyonun ilgili güvenlik birimlerinin yürüttüğü istihbarat toplama, araştırma, takip ve koordinasyon çalışmaları sonucunda gerçekleştirildiğini aktardı.

Avrupa Birliğinin (AB) Yemen Delegasyonu yaptığı açıklamada, Avrupa Komisyonunun 2026 yılı için daha önce ayırdığı 87,5 milyon avroya ek olarak Yemen'e 22,5 milyon avroluk insani yardım tahsis ettiğini bildirdi.

Açıklamada, yeni yardımın çatışmaların yeniden şiddetlenmesinin yol açtığı etkileri hafifletmeyi ve milyonlarca kişinin acil insani ihtiyaçlarını karşılamayı amaçladığı belirtildi.

AB Delegasyonu, askeri gerilimin geniş çaplı yerinden edilmelere, sivil kayıpların artmasına ve temel hizmetler ile insani yardımlara erişimdeki zorlukların derinleşmesine neden olduğunu kaydetti.

Anadolu Ajansı web sitesinde, AA Haber Akış Sistemi (HAS) üzerinden abonelere sunulan haberler, özetlenerek yayımlanmaktadır.Abonelik için lütfen iletişime geçiniz.

Bu haberde bir hata mı var? Her türlü talep, şikâyet ve görüş için okur temsilcimize ulaşabilirsiniz.

#3254. Flying Bat Cups STEM Challenge

These flying cups are a great STEM challenge for any time of year. One cup acts as an elastic band launcher, and the other is launched into the air! It’s a fun way to learn about forces, motion, elasticity and gravity!

When the cup to be launched pushes down on the elastic bands, they become stretched, storing elastic potential energy. When the cup is released, the potential energy in the elastic bands is converted into kinetic energy, which is then transferred to the flying cup, launching it into the air.

Using a sharp pencil, carefully poke four holes in the cup as shown below. Ask an adult to help.

Cut the elastic bands into a single, long strip.

Thread the elastic bands through the holes in the cup as shown and secure the ends with tape. This is the mechanism that will catapult the paper cup bats into the air. Put this cup inside another cup (this is just so it looks better and is not necessary).

Decorate a second paper cup to look like a bat or other Halloween theme.

Push a decorated cup into a cup with elastic bands and let go.

When the elastic bands in the launcher cup are stretched, energy is stored in the bands. We call this elastic potential energy. The more the elastic band is stretched, the more potential energy it has.

The elastic potential energy stored in the stretched bands is first converted into kinetic energy as the bands contract. This kinetic energy is then transferred to the paper cup, launching it into the air.

Take care not to stretch the elastic bands too much, as if they are stretched beyond their elastic limit, they become damaged and won’t spring back.

If you enjoyed this activity, don’t forget to give my other Halloween STEM challenges a try.

#3255. Long Presumed Dead, a West African Reef Is Found Thriving

This article was originally published by Inside Climate News and is reproduced here as part of the Climate Desk collaboration.

Soft corals found off the coast of Benin. Gérard Zinzindohoué / IRHOB

Sixty years after surveyors first uncovered evidence of a sprawling coral reef off the West African nation of Benin, scientists have at last found the reef.

In the 1960s, fishing surveyors off West Africa’s Benin coast hauled up heads of coral in their nets. Employed by local governments to assess fish diversity and discover potentially trawlable seabeds in the sandy-bottomed Gulf of Guinea, the researchers shrugged off the discovery at the time, burying the find in a brief paragraph in a 130-something-page report.

With no surveys since, the scientists who followed had lost track of exactly where the reefs might lie. And as mass bleaching events and overfishing slashed the world’s coral reef area by more than 50 percent since the 1950s, local oceanographers had written off any remnants of a possible reef as dead.

More than six decades on, the mystery has been solved as a team of Beninese scientists rediscovered the healthy reef teeming with marine life. At least eight coral types and eight fish species have formed a thriving ecosystem on this long-forgotten site.

“I was guided by hope. A hope that sometimes there are species or ecosystems which kind of defeat time, or are trying to resist it,” said Gérard Zinzindohoué, the project lead for Coral Reefs Rediscovering & Exploration in Benin.

Fresh off seeing coral for the first time in Cape Verde in 2021, Zinzindohoué asked himself a simple question: Do we have any reefs in Benin?

After a colleague passed on the original 1960s report — which mapped out a potential 24-mile-long coral reef barrier along Benin’s coastline — Zinzindohoué recognized a gap in the scientific knowledge: “I realized how little we know about our own coastal environment.”

But in the years that followed, a string of failed grant applications halted progress on solving the mystery.

When he secured a $20,000 National Geographic Explorers grant in January 2025, the project finally ignited. Yet getting the gear was also challenging. The sonar equipment swallowed up almost 80 percent of the grant budget and nearly stalled entirely when the European supplier refused to accept payment from Zinzindohoué’s African bank account.

To complicate matters further, Benin doesn’t have a permanent research vessel. Zinzindohoué therefore had no choice but to seek the help of local fishermen to ferry the team to the suspected site 14 miles offshore and jury-rig the pirogue to accommodate towing the high-tech sonar device.

“The fishermen’s boats are not used to going that far offshore, so it was risky for everyone,” said Zinzindohoué, whose team battled repeated engine failures and months of nausea-filled fieldwork before finally locating two strong sonar echoes pinging from the depths below. National Geographic — whom Zinzindohoué credits with the success of the project — then dispatched a high-resolution deep-sea camera system from their Exploration Technology Lab to investigate the sonar results.

Footage of the coral reef off the coast of Benin. Gérard Zinzindohoué / IRHOB

While their wooden pirogue bounced atop the offshore swell, the team filmed the seafloor below but had to wait until they returned to dry land to scour the footage. What they had captured was a wealth of marine life: six types of soft coral; two black corals; and eight species of sheltering fish, from golden African snappers to the Monrovia doctorfish.

When the first images came through, Zinzindohoué texted Houangninan Midinoudewa, an oceanographic researcher at the Benin Marine Conservation Club and the same friend who first told Zinzindohoué of the 1960s report.

Midinoudewa remembers the moment: “I said, ‘Bro, 60 years later we are now discovering exactly what we have in our water.’ We found it still alive, producing more fish for communities and supporting livelihoods. I was really joyful and really overwhelmed.”

While no coral samples have yet been extracted, researchers have classified the site as a mesophotic coral ecosystem (MCE). Such systems are light-dependent communities existing at the lower limits of reef-building corals. At more than 175 feet below the surface, the coral garden they discovered appears patchily scattered on a rocky substrate.

Bridging the gap between shallow reefs and deeper benthic habitats, MCEs are home to distinct species and unique environmental conditions. While scientists are increasingly recognizing their ecological and climatic importance, they remain among the least explored elements of tropical and subtropical marine biodiversity. And this one has real potential to unlock new information about coral history.

“Since it’s an undisturbed marine ecosystem, it can help through carbon dating or paleoclimatic study to tell us which kind of climate system has occurred here in the past,” said Zinzindohoué. “It’s better to know the past to help explain the present, and help know which kind of direction we can take in the future.”

In addition to the blackbar soldierfish, West African goatfish, and Guinean angelfish filmed darting through the reef, the discovery presents potential conservation claims for other marine life.

“We will be advocating for its full protection, perhaps by setting up a marine protected area around it,” said Midinoudewa, who specializes in elasmobranchs — the study of sharks, rays, and skates.

Midinoudewa is currently submitting an application to designate the area as an Important Shark and Ray Area with the International Union for Conservation of Nature. Based on the knowledge of local fishermen, Midinoudewa is confident the reef is home to sawback angel sharks, silky sharks, brown skates, and marbled stingrays.

The team behind the discovery hopes this will spark a wave of similar discoveries in the waters off West Africa, an area of the world underserved by scientific research projects

“I hope the Gulf of Guinea will be a hub for research because we know our resources are being exploited and people [need to] know exactly what we have and why we should care,” said Midinoudewa.

Zinzindohoué agrees: “We don’t need to wait for others to come to our country to show us what is under our sea. We are the ones who must take responsibility.”

Efforts to Save Kelp Forests from Ocean Warming Are Ramping Up

#3258. Pixel 11 Pro review: Google’s best pocket camera goes customisable

Guardian journalism is independent. We earn a commission if you buy something through an affiliate link. Learn more.

Solid battery life, snappy performance and quality software make for a great smaller phone with a killer camera

Google’s latest Pro phone is out to prove it has the best camera on a smartphone while embracing customisation, allowing you to change the look and feel of your photos far beyond simple filters even if that means making them technically worse.

The Pixel 11 Pro packs the best of Google’s hardware and software into a still-pocketable and easy to handle frame, instantly making it a contender for best smaller phone of the year.

Being smaller doesn’t make it cheap. The 11 Pro sits above the regular £879 Pixel 11 and costs from £1,079 (€1,199/$1,099/A$1,849) – £80 (€100/$100/A$150) more than last year, with double the storage but less memory owing to the cost of RAMageddon.

Little has changed on the outside for the Pro Pixels in several years. The new model is 0.1mm smaller in all directions and 3g lighter but has the same size 6.3in OLED screen, same polished aluminium and glass body and a large camera bar sticking out the back. It is an attractive design that has aged well.

The temperature sensor from previous Pixel Pro generations, which always felt like a gimmick, has been replaced by a coloured HiLight LED that works as a camera flash and notification light. The LED glows when you talk to Gemini or pulses with one of five colours when your favourite contact calls. But it doesn’t work for notifications of any other kind, so is a wasted opportunity.

The 11 Pro has the same Tensor G6 chip as the regular Pixel 11 and has 12 or 16GB of RAM depending on the storage version. It can’t match the raw performance of the top Qualcomm or Apple chips for games but is snappy in day-to-day tasks.

Battery life is just shy of two days with the screen actively used for about seven hours across a mix of wifi and 5G for browsing, messaging apps and photos. This is a bit behind the regular Pixel 11 but should see out even the heaviest of use days with some left in the tank. Most people will need to charge the 11 Pro every other day for general use.

Camera: 50MP + 48MP UW + 48MP 5x tele; 42MP selfie

Connectivity: 5G, eSIM, wifi 7, UWB, NFC, Bluetooth 6, Thread and GNSS

Water resistance: IP68 (1.5m for 30 minutes)

The 11 Pro runs the same Google version of Android 17 as the regular Pixel 11 and will be supported until August 2033 with operating system and security updates, making it one of the longest-lasting devices available.

It’s a polished experience with smooth animations, nice touches of haptic feedback across the system and enough customisation to make it yours. But as with other Pixel devices, the phone is a showcase for Google’s most advanced AI tools that are littered throughout the software.

The 11 Pro comes with six months of Google AI Pro subscription, which gives you access to Gemini Omni to generate surprisingly good eight-second videos, higher AI usage limits, 5TB of cloud storage and Google Health Premium.

But the most interesting tools are the local AI ones, including the new Rambler voice dictation feature in Gboard, which produces fully formed succinct sentences and paragraphs from your ramblings, removing unnecessary pauses, ums, ers and deviations. Gemini’s proactive assistance has also improved with more time-saving suggestions, although it only works in certain apps and not WhatsApp, Signal or other third-party apps.

The reason you buy a Pixel Pro is for the camera. The 11 Pro has a very good 42-megapixel selfie camera on the front and a similar winning triple camera system on the rear from last year: a 50MP wide, 48MP ultra wide and a 48MP 5x telephoto camera.

All three cameras capture really fantastic photos across a range of lighting conditions with very little effort. The wide and telephoto cameras now capture more light and slightly better detail, but the biggest boost is the new Instant Night Sight. It dramatically speeds up low-light photos by up to 4.5 times, which makes taking great shots in very dark scenes a lot easier.

The 5x telephoto camera manages a very good 10x crop zoom before stretching up to 120x magnification, first with a very good traditional digital zoom to 30x then using local generative AI to go beyond. The camera keeps the regular digital zoom image and the one processed by AI to add back lost detail, which can be remarkable or filled with artefacts.

Magic Capture takes video and photos automatically with just one button for point-and-shoot recording, which works well for events such as a birthday party.

But Camera Looks is the most interesting feature for getting creative with photography. It allows you to modify how the camera processes your photos, starting with subtle changes such as darker shadows and ranging to more distinctive looks such as “minimal”, which reduces the computational photography processing for a more raw photo. Other options include “digi”, which replicates the look of old digital cameras that have become popular, and “velvet”, which mimics the look of some film cameras. Each look can be further customised, allowing great creative licence.

The Pixel shoots great video with lots of stabilisation and processing tools including the new “Creator Suite”, which captures clips straight into a folder for storyboarding and trimming, with a teleprompter, framing for social media and speed enhancement.

The battery is rated to last in excess of 1,000 full-charge cycles with at least 80% of its original capacity. The phone is repairable by Google, third-party shops or self-repair with manuals and parts available.

The Pixel 11 Pro contains 31% recycled materials by weight. The company breaks down the phone’s environmental impact in its report and will recycle old devices for free.

The Google Pixel 11 Pro costs from £1,079 (€1,199/$1,099/A$1,849) in a choice of four colours.

For comparison, the Pixel 11 costs £879, Pixel 11 Pro XL costs £1,279, Pixel 11 Pro Fold costs from £1,799, the Pixel 10a costs £499, the Samsung Galaxy S26 costs £879, the Samsung Galaxy S26 Ultra costs £1,279 and the Apple iPhone 17 Pro costs £1,099.

The Pixel 11 Pro is a refinement of multiple generations of Google’s Pro phones rather than an outright revolution, but that’s no bad thing.

It is one of the very best smaller phones, packing all the top-end hardware, cameras and AI features of Google’s most biggest and expensive model into a slightly cheaper and more pocketable form. You buy a Pro Pixel for the camera, which doesn’t disappoint with arguably the best photography on a phone at the moment.

The ability to customise the processing of your photos goes far beyond rivals for interesting creative potential. The dramatically faster Night Sight is also a significant improvement.

Gemini’s proactive tools show great potential but depend on using the right apps. The supercharged Rambler voice dictation system is fantastic, and there are many other little AI tools that are useful from time to time.

With just shy of two days battery life, a decent chip and quality software, the Pixel 11 Pro is a great pocketable phone. It just isn’t a massive upgrade on the already excellent Pixel 11 or recent Pixel Pro models.

Pros: great camera with extended zoom and extensive customisation, seven years of software updates, excellent screen, impressive dictation and local AI features, Qi2 wireless charging and magnetic accessory support, good battery life, great size, fast fingerprint and face recognition, six months of Google AI Pro included.

Cons: quite expensive, less memory on cheapest model than before, face unlock option not as secure as Face ID, raw performance and battery life short of best-in-class, no physical sim card slot in the US, not a massive upgrade.

#3260. Erneute Wahlverschiebung in Guinea-Bissau: Politisches Chaos hält an

Die für den 6. Dezember angekündigten Wahlen in Guinea-Bissau sind auf unbestimmte Zeit verschoben. Ein neuer Termin steht nicht fest. Die Rückkehr zu einer zivilen Regierung bleibt ungewiss.

Die für den 6. Dezember 2026 angekündigten Parlaments- und Präsidentschaftswahlen in Guinea-Bissau finden nicht wie geplant statt. Aly Silva, Sprecher des Übergangs-Premierministers Ilídio Vieira Té, bestätigte der DW, dass die Abstimmung bis auf Weiteres verschoben wird. Ein neuer Termin steht nicht fest. Zunächst wolle sich die Regierung im Kabinett beraten und anschließend öffentlich mitteilen, wann gewählt werden soll. Ob die Wahlen 2027 oder erst 2028 stattfinden, ist offen.

Die politische Krise geht auf den 26. November 2025 zurück. Drei Tage zuvor hatten die Menschen in Guinea-Bissau ein neues Parlament und einen Präsidenten gewählt. Noch bevor die offiziellen Ergebnisse bekannt gegeben wurden, übernahm das Militär die Macht und setzte den Wahlprozess außer Kraft. Sowohl der damalige Präsident Umaro Sissoco Embaló als auch sein Herausforderer Fernando Dias da Costa hatten den Wahlsieg für sich beansprucht.

Die Militärführung hat eine Rückkehr zur verfassungsmäßigen Ordnung versprochen. Doch die angekündigten Neuwahlen rücken erneut in die Ferne. Für den Juristen und politischen Analysten Cabi Sanhá kommt die Verschiebung nicht überraschend. Nach dem Putsch sei kaum etwas unternommen worden, um die Voraussetzungen für glaubwürdige Wahlen zu schaffen.

"Die Verschiebung war absehbar", sagt Sanhá im DW-Interview. Zwar sei der 6. Dezember als neuer Wahltermin festgelegt worden. "Aber es wurde nichts getan, damit diese Wahlen tatsächlich stattfinden können."

Die Übergangsregierung begründet die Verschiebung mit den fehlenden organisatorischen und politischen Voraussetzungen. Die Frist für die Einreichung der Kandidaturen lief bereits am 6. Oktober ab. Die Parteien und möglichen Kandidaten hatten ihre Bewerbungen nicht eingereicht. Nach Angaben des Premierministers hatten die Parteien zuvor auch keine Kongresse abgehalten, um ihre Kandidaten zu bestimmen.

Sanhá sieht darin nicht allein ein organisatorisches Problem. Er verweist auf die Einschränkungen politischer Aktivitäten und den fehlenden Dialog mit den zentralen Akteuren des Wahlprozesses. Parteien und Zivilgesellschaft seien nicht ausreichend einbezogen worden. Auch die Vermittlungsbemühungen der Westafrikanischen Wirtschaftsgemeinschaft ECOWAS und Senegals hätten bislang keine Lösung gebracht.

"Freie, transparente und inklusive Wahlen sind nur möglich, wenn man zuvor die Voraussetzungen dafür schafft", sagt der Jurist. Dass Parteien ihre Kandidaten nicht aufgestellt haben, betrachtet er deshalb auch als Folge der politischen Rahmenbedingungen. "Wie sollen Parteien Kandidaten aufstellen, wenn politische Aktivitäten im Land eingeschränkt sind? Sie können weder ihre Parteitage abhalten noch Versammlungen organisieren."

Führende Oppositionspolitiker halten sich zudem im Ausland auf. Domingos Simões Pereira, Vorsitzender der größten Oppositionspartei PAIGC, durfte im Juli 2026 aus der Haft nach Portugal reisen, um sich medizinisch behandeln zu lassen. Er war zuvor im Zusammenhang mit Ermittlungen zu einem mutmaßlichen Putschversuch in Untersuchungshaft genommen worden. Deine Verteidigung bestreitet die Vorwürfe und kritisiert das Verfahren als "politisch motiviert".

Die Abwesenheit wichtiger Oppositionsvertreter und die eingeschränkten Möglichkeiten politischer Organisation erschweren einen Wahlprozess, der eigentlich den Übergang zu einer zivilen Regierung ermöglichen sollte.

Wie es nun weitergeht, ist unklar. Nach Angaben des Regierungsmitarbeiters Aly Silva will sich die Übergangsregierung zunächst im Kabinett beraten. Erst anschließend soll ein neuer Wahltermin öffentlich bekannt gegeben werden. Einen konkreten Zeitplan gibt es bislang nicht.

Damit bleibt auch offen, wann Parteien ihre internen Verfahren nachholen, Kandidaten nominieren und den Wahlkampf aufnehmen können. Für Sanhá ist das ein Zeichen dafür, dass der politische Wille zur Rückkehr zur verfassungsmäßigen Ordnung bislang nicht ausreichend erkennbar sei.

"Ich glaube nicht, dass es in erster Linie an logistischen Problemen liegt. Vielmehr fehlt es offenbar am politischen Willen, den Wahlprozess voranzubringen", sagt er.

Die politische Krise wird durch eine umstrittene Verfassungsreform verschärft. Bei einem Referendum am 30. August stimmte eine Mehrheit für Verfassungsänderungen, die die Befugnisse des Präsidenten erweitern und das Parlament von 102 auf 65 Sitze verkleinern. Kritiker bezweifeln die Legitimität des Verfahrens, weil die Reform unter einer Militärführung und ohne ein regulär gewähltes Parlament vorbereitet wurde. Die Opposition hatte zum Boykott der Abstimmung aufgerufen.

Fernando Vaz, Sprecher des Nationalen Übergangsrats, verteidigt die Änderungen. Es habe sich nicht um eine umfassende Neufassung, sondern um eine punktuelle Überarbeitung der Verfassung gehandelt. Die Reform solle helfen, die politische Instabilität des Landes zu überwinden.

"Die Verfassung muss eines der Instrumente sein, die unser Land zu mehr Stabilität führen", sagte Vaz im DW-Interview. Die Übergangszeit habe eine Gelegenheit geboten, Regelungen zu ändern, die aus Sicht der Militärführung die politische Entwicklung behinderten. Vaz weist zudem die Kritik zurück, die Reform übertrage dem Präsidenten zusätzliche Macht auf Kosten des Premierministers. "Der Präsident der Republik ist Staatsoberhaupt, der Premierminister ist Regierungschef", betont er.

Kritiker sehen das anders - und verweisen darauf, dass die neue Verfassungsordnung die Stellung des Präsidenten stärke: Er soll den Premierminister und die Regierung ernennen und entlassen sowie unter bestimmten Voraussetzungen das Parlament auflösen können. Zugleich wird die Zahl der Parlamentssitze reduziert. Damit bleibt umstritten, ob die Reform tatsächlich zur Stabilisierung beiträgt oder die Macht stärker bei der Präsidentschaft konzentriert.

Die Übergangsregierung hat angekündigt, 2027 Kommunalwahlen abzuhalten. Sanhá begrüßt das grundsätzlich: Seit der Unabhängigkeit habe Guinea-Bissau noch keine solchen Wahlen durchgeführt. Doch sie dürften die ausstehenden nationalen Wahlen nicht ersetzen, mahnt er: "Zuerst müssen die Parlaments- und Präsidentschaftswahlen stattfinden, damit das Land zur verfassungsmäßigen Ordnung zurückkehren kann. Danach können die Kommunalwahlen folgen."

Die lokale Ebene könne die fehlende demokratische Legitimation auf nationaler Ebene nicht ausgleichen. Zudem müssten politische Freiheiten wiederhergestellt werden, damit Parteien ihre Kandidaten bestimmen könnten und die Bevölkerung tatsächlich zwischen politischen Alternativen wählen könne.

Guinea-Bissau blickt auf Jahrzehnte politischer Instabilität zurück, geprägt von Militärputschen, institutionellen Krisen und wiederholten Unterbrechungen demokratischer Prozesse. Der jüngste Putsch setzt diese Entwicklung fort.

Die erneute Verschiebung der Wahlen zeigt, wie weit das Land noch von einer Rückkehr zu einer verfassungsmäßigen Ordnung entfernt ist. Für Jurist Cabi Sanhá ist klar: Ein neuer Wahltermin allein wird nicht genügen. Dafür, so Sanhá, müssten politische Aktivitäten wieder zugelassen, die Parteien in den Prozess einbezogen und verlässliche Rahmenbedingungen geschaffen werden.

#3263. Googlebook review: Google’s new laptops are woefully underbaked

Guardian journalism is independent. We earn a commission if you buy something through an affiliate link. Learn more.

Great hardware let down by software flaws on these pricey machines that combine ChromeOS with Android

Google’s attempt to create a new type of laptop to compete with Windows and macOS machines is finally here, combining the best of ChromeOS and Android into the new Googlebook. With advanced links to Android phones and the best of Google built in, can these Googlebooks really compete with a MacBook Air or Surface Laptop? I took two for a spin to find out.

For years, Chromebooks have been the boss of budget laptops, but Googlebooks take a different, far more premium approach. Made by a range of traditional PC makers, including Acer, Asus, Dell, HP and Lenovo, they start at £999 (€1,099/$899/A$2,299) – a far cry from the £160 Chromebooks.

While Google does not make these machines directly, it has set strict hardware quality standards that the laptops must reach. That includes backlit keyboards, haptic trackpads, fingerprint readers, crisp touchscreens, decent speakers and modern fast chips with at least 16GB of RAM.

It is a list of specs you should expect with a premium Windows PC, but those start at a much higher price, primarily thanks to RAMageddon.

As such, the Acer Googlebook 14 and the Asus Googlebook 14 I’ve been testing have excellent hardware with satisfying keyboards, great trackpads and quality screens.

The Acer is a convertible that allows the screen to fold over the back of the device turning into a tablet of sorts. It is 1.14kg and 13.9mm thick, with a glossy 14in 3K OLED screen and a smooth aluminium-magnesium body, costing £999.99.

The Asus is a traditional laptop with a matt 14in 3K OLED screen and a magnesium-alloy body that weighs just 0.99kg and is 11mm thick, costing £1,199.99.

Top quality hardware aside, anyone familiar with a Chromebook would be forgiven for thinking it was ChromeOS running on these machines. It has the familiar taskbar at the bottom, Chrome-based web apps and access to Android apps.

But the software is essentially Android underneath, which means getting it up and running is just like setting up an Android phone: scan the QR code with your phone, enter a passcode and transfer your apps and accounts. It is all done and dusted in about four minutes, which is faster than setting up a modern Windows 11 machine even if you know all the shortcuts.

Access to the full gamut of Android apps is the Googlebook’s biggest strength and its greatest weakness. Practically any app you have on your phone can be installed and run on the laptop. But almost all those phone apps are just that, mobile versions, with most not even aware that they are not installed on a phone.

Those that have at least some tailoring to the laptop form come in a few different types, categorised by Google broadly under “compatible with desktop”, which means they should support mouse and keyboard controls; “optimised for desktop”, which means they should scale properly to the laptop’s display; and “made for desktop”, which are a handful of Android apps redesigned with the laptop in mind.

Only the last category really seems to make any difference to the quality of the app, and even then most of them are still not of the same calibre as their equivalents found on Windows or macOS.

Even Google’s apps are a mixed bag. Google Calendar can only show one account at any one time, so you can’t see your personal, work and shared calendars on one screen like you can on Android.

Google Docs starts as its own app, but when you actually open a document it just becomes another Chrome window hidden behind the Chrome logo on the task bar, not the Docs logo.

The Files app shows your Google Drive files, but you can’t pin locations to the side bar to quickly access your commonly used folders, making it a time-consuming dig through endless folders to find things each time.

Even the Chrome browser is not quite up to par with its Windows or macOS counterpart, lacking features such as split-screen tabs, the auto-hiding vertical tab bar or the ability to have multiple profiles, such as a personal and work account.

Google has an app-gap problem and one that will only be solved if developers are convinced Googlebook OS is going to take off and be worth spending time and money making better apps for.

The other big selling point is deep integration with your Android phone. Google calls this “better together”, which is a collection of features that mirror capabilities of iPhones with Macs or Microsoft’s Link to Windows.

Some of the features are like magic. You can search the files on your phone straight from the Files app on the laptop without plugging it in, see your notifications from your phone, continue tasks on your laptop from your phone and even use apps cast from your phone.

As useful as these features sound, many are not up to scratch. Streaming apps from your phone is so slow that I feel as though I can type entire sentences before they appear on screen in chat apps, and it is restricted to one app at a time. If you click on a synced notification from your phone, it will open in a cast app, even if you have the full app installed on your Googlebook. Even viewing files on your phone from the laptop is irritatingly slow.

The only apps I’ve reliably managed to get to appear with the ability to continue from where I was are Google Docs and Chrome on the phone.

It also took me two hours of troubleshooting to get an up-to-date Google Pixel 11 Pro to actually connect with the Googlebooks, despite using the phone to set them up in the first place.

The other big selling point for Google is its Gemini integration into Googlebook OS. The Gemini app is just like it is on macOS and Windows. But it is the Magic Pointer feature, which appears when you wiggle the mouse to click or highlight something on your screen to send it to Gemini, that is the most novel.

You can use it to get Gemini to summarise text, explain something, search for things or generally chat. But I often hit a road block, such as Gemini refusing to generate images or saying it cannot add a date into my calendar in the mode Magic Cursor opens, which oddly isn’t within the desktop Gemini app. Sometimes it works, sometimes it doesn’t, and it is simply not joined up.

Magic Cue has been imported from Google’s Pixel phones, which is meant to pop up with helpful information such as calendar availability or a location in maps when you highlight a date or a place in a chat or email. But unlike on a Pixel phone, I have yet to get it to successfully activate in two weeks of testing.

Google’s super-charged voice dictation system called Rambler, also from the recent Pixels, does at least work as described. Press the dictation key and just speak naturally. Rambler will condense your speech into concise sentences removing filler words and errors.

Googlebooks are full of excellent ideas about how a modern laptop should work. The hardware of the Acer and Asus machines I’ve tested is top notch, but the software just isn’t ready for prime time.

When it works it’s great, but it is filled with limitations that might be acceptable on a super-cheap laptop but never on a £1,000 premium machine.

Chrome is not as good as it is on a Mac or Windows PC. Google’s various apps are generally better on an Android phone and the third-party app ecosystem is a mess of poorly optimised mobile apps. Even the best desktop-optimised Android apps pale by comparison to their counterparts on Windows or macOS.

The Android phone-linking system should be the killer feature, but it is partly broken or simply not up to scratch. Even the Gemini integration is flakey.

Most of the Google-specific issues and software bugs are eminently fixable. But the app gap will take serious time, effort and money to convince third-party developers to fix.

Unless you want to spend at least £1,000 to be a beta tester, your money is better spent elsewhere for now.

#3265. In Appalachia, Severe Floods Linked to Spikes in Opioid Deaths

The aftermath of flooding in Clendenin, West Virginia, in June 2016. Ty Wright / Getty Images

As global warming fuels more extreme rainfall in Appalachia, new research finds a link between flooding and opioid deaths.

Across the U.S., the heaviest downpours are becoming more severe, giving rise to more intense floods. The new study, which analyzed flooding and mortality data from counties across Appalachia, found surges in lethal overdoses following severe floods, with the effects persisting for up to a decade. On average, yearly opioid deaths rose by 26 percent in the year or two after a flood, according to the study, which was published in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences.

From 2000 to 2017, there were a total of 11,744 opioid overdose deaths across rural Appalachia, said co-lead author Kristina Brant, of Penn State University. “We estimate that severe floods led to 1,368 of those overdose deaths.”

In interviews undertaken as part of the study, health care providers told researchers that people affected by severe floods are vulnerable to using drugs to cope with the loss of their home or their job, or simply the trauma of living through a disaster. At the same time, flooding can make it harder to access treatment for addiction, as prior research has shown.

According to the new study, the spike in opioid deaths was most pronounced among those living in poorer areas and those with no more than a high school education. Said Brant, “it is more vulnerable people and communities that seem to face the largest impacts.”

#3266. Fizzy Foam Investigation

A baking soda and vinegar volcano eruption or a fizzy potion are classic childhood science experiments and a great way to introduce the concept of chemical reactions. Washing-up liquid ( dish soap ) is often used to make the foam thicker and longer-lasting, but we recently discovered that egg white is a good alternative!

This Halloween themed investigation compares dish soap, egg white and extra water to discover which combination makes the best fizzy foam!

Pour a small amount of food colouring into each container.

Add the same amount of water to each container so the total amount of liquid is about one-fifth of the container.

Add a tablespoon of washing up liquid to one container, water to another and egg white to the third.

Add a heaped teaspoon of bicarbonate of soda to each and stir well.

Pour about 25ml of vinegar into each container and observe the reaction.

The amount of each substance isn’t important, but you should add the same amount of each to each container to make it a fair test.

We found that the foam without washing up liquid or egg white was thin and didn’t last long. The egg white foam was the most foamy and thicker than the washing-up liquid version.

Baking soda and vinegar react to neutralise each other. Baking soda is an alkali, and vinegar is an acid. Carbon dioxide gas is given off, which is why the mixture fizzes upwards. When just water is used, the bubbles pop quickly, resulting in thin foam that disappears quickly.

Washing up liquid contacts surfactants, which lower the surface tension of water. They create a thick, stretchy skin around the bubbles, allowing them to last longer. Surfactants in washing-up liquid help break down grease and make the mixture more foamy.

Egg white is about 10% protein. When the mixture containing egg white is mixed, the proteins unfold and surround the bubbles of gas to form a stable network around them. This leads to the foamy mixture you see.

Discover why egg yolks are left out of meringue with a fun science challenge!

Find out how to bake ice cream in an oven without it melting. A Baked Alaska is a delicious science based dessert!

Egg whites give lift to foods such as soufflés and meringue, as when they are whisked, the proteins unfold and trap air bubbles!

Egg whites contain over 150 different proteins.

#3268. Cold activates a BH2 metabolic network promoting longevity and proteostasis

Thank you for visiting nature.com. You are using a browser version with limited support for CSS. To obtain the best experience, we recommend you use a more up to date browser (or turn off compatibility mode in Internet Explorer). In the meantime, to ensure continued support, we are displaying the site without styles and JavaScript.

Nature (2026) Cite this article

Moderately cold temperatures extend lifespan and promote health across species1,2,3, yet the molecular mechanisms underlying these benefits remain unclear. Here we define a metabolite-driven, multi-tissue signalling system that links cold temperature to enhanced longevity and proteostasis in Caenorhabditis elegans. Cold temperature upregulates the biopterin-synthesis enzyme CAT-4 (GCH1 in humans), increasing production of dihydrobiopterin (BH2), a transportable reservoir for the bioactive cofactor tetrahydrobiopterin (BH4). BH2 is produced in muscle and transported to distal tissues, including the intestine and neurons, where it is converted into BH4 by the salvage enzyme DHFR-1. BH4 functions as an essential cofactor for phenylalanine hydroxylase PAH-1, promoting tyrosine synthesis and extending lifespan. This BH2–BH4 axis also prevents disease-related protein aggregation and subsequent neuronal dysfunction during ageing. Importantly, increasing BH2 synthesis specifically in muscle is sufficient to promote longevity and suppress pathological protein aggregation in distal tissues, even at normal or elevated temperatures. In human cells, BH2 or tyrosine supplementation reduces aggregation of polyQ-expanded proteins associated with Huntington’s disease, as well as mutant variants of TDP-43 and FUS that cause amyotrophic lateral sclerosis. Together, these findings identify BH2 as a cold-induced metabolite of which the synthesis, intertissue transport and conversion into bioactive BH4 preserve organism-wide proteostasis and extend lifespan.

Emerging evidence demonstrates that moderately cold temperatures delay ageing and promote health across species1. Lowering body temperature extends lifespan in both poikilotherms, including C. elegans, Drosophila melanogaster and various fish species, and homeotherms, including rodents1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8. In adult C. elegans, a shift from standard (20 °C) to moderately low temperature (15 °C) markedly extends lifespan2,9,10,11,12. In rodents, a mild decrease in body temperature by only around 0.5 °C prolongs lifespan, whereas a comparable increase shortens it3,4. Lower body temperature also correlates with longevity in humans13,14. Notably, average human body temperature has declined since the Industrial Revolution, raising the possibility that changes in body temperature may have contributed to increased longevity over recent decades13. Together, these findings suggest that moderate cold engages evolutionarily conserved programs that promote longevity.

Despite being recognized for more than a century15, the mechanisms by which moderate cold temperature promotes longevity remain largely unclear. Historically, cold-induced lifespan extension was interpreted as a consequence of passive thermodynamic effects, including reduced metabolic rate and slower biochemical reaction kinetics. However, studies in C. elegans revealed that cold-induced longevity is an actively regulated process2,9,10,11,12. For example, the temperature-sensitive TRPA-1 ion channel promotes longevity at 15–20 °C but not at warmer temperatures, indicating that low temperature is sensed and transduced into longevity mechanisms11,12. Moreover, cold temperature induces transcriptional programs that enhance proteostasis2,9.

Despite these advances, a fundamental question remains of what systemic signals coordinate cold-induced responses across tissues. Small metabolites are ideally suited for this role because they can act as rapidly diffusible regulators integrating metabolism, enzymatic activity and intertissue signalling. We therefore hypothesized that cold temperature induces metabolite-based signals that coordinate metabolic, proteostatic and longevity pathways across tissues. We further hypothesized that such metabolites could function as temperature-independent effectors capable of recapitulating the beneficial effects of cold temperature even at higher temperatures.

Here we identify BH2 as a cold-induced metabolite of which the synthesis, intertissue transport and conversion into the bioactive enzymatic cofactor BH4 constitute a multi-tissue regulatory system that promotes longevity and maintains proteostasis across tissues. Through metabolomic, genetic and physiological analyses, we find that elevated BH2 synthesis in muscle and its conversion to BH4 in distal tissues promotes tyrosine synthesis, thereby mediating key aspects of cold-induced longevity. This pathway can be leveraged to confer longevity and proteostasis benefits at normal and elevated temperatures in both C. elegans and human cells.

Exposure of C. elegans to moderately low temperature (15 °C) after development induces a considerable extension of lifespan2,10,11,12. To assess metabolic changes associated with this effect, we raised wild-type worms at the standard temperature (20 °C) until adulthood and subsequently transferred them to 15 °C, 20 °C or 25 °C. Untargeted metabolomics analysis on day 5 of adulthood identified 64 metabolites that were significantly increased at 15 °C relative to both 20 °C and 25 °C (Fig. 1a and Supplementary Table 1). An independent experiment confirmed 45 of these metabolites, with strong concordance between datasets (Extended Data Fig. 1a–d and Supplementary Table 2).

a, Metabolomics of day 5 adult wild-type worms at 15 °C versus 20 °C (left) or 25 °C (right). n = 5 biological replicates. The volcano plots show log2-transformed fold change (log2[FC]) versus −log10-transformed FDR-adjusted P value (−log10[q]). The red dots indicate the 45 cold-induced metabolites increased at 15 °C versus both 20 °C and 25 °C in two independent experiments (FDR-adjusted P < 0.01; Supplementary Tables 1 and 2). b, Schematic of de novo, salvage and recycling BH4 synthesis pathways. Cold-induced metabolites are shown in red font; key enzymes required for cold-induced longevity are outlined by red boxes. C. elegans lacks sepiapterin reductase (SPR). The diagram was created using BioRender; Vilchez, D. https://BioRender.com/mno2d7j (2026). c, BH2 levels in adult wild-type worms across ages at 20 °C or 15 °C. Data are mean ± s.e.m. n = 5 biological replicates. d, BH2 levels in day 5 adult wild-type and trpa-1(ok999) mutant worms. Data are mean ± s.e.m. n = 5 biological replicates. e, Quantitative proteomics of day 5 adult wild-type worms at 15 °C versus 20 °C (above) or 25 °C (below). n = 3 biological replicates. The red dots indicate proteins commonly increased at 15 °C versus 20 °C and 25 °C (FDR < 0.05). f, BH4 levels in day 5 adult sterile fer-15;fem-1 worms treated after development with vector or cat-4 RNAi at 15 °C or 20 °C. Data are mean ± s.e.m., relative to 20 °C + vector RNAi. n = 8 independent experiments. g, cat-4 RNAi after development shortens the lifespan of wild-type worms at 15 °C (P < 0.0001) but not at 20 °C (P = 0.2595) or 25 °C (P = 0.8993). h, dhfr-1 RNAi after development shortens lifespan at 15 °C (P = 0.0005) but not at 20 °C (P = 0.7861). For g,h, n = 96 worms per condition; data are representative of two independent experiments. Data are mean ± s.e.m. and replicate data from independent lifespan experiments are provided in Supplementary Table 7. Statistical analysis was performed using two-sided t-tests with FDR correction (a,e), two-way analysis of variance (ANOVA) with Tukey’s multiple-comparison test (c,d) or Fisher’s least significant difference (LSD) test (f), and two-sided log-rank tests (g,h).

Among the 45 reproducibly cold-induced metabolites, several were linked to BH4 synthesis and its function as an enzymatic cofactor, including GTP, BH2, tryptophan, phenylalanine and tyrosine (Fig. 1a,b). As BH2 was among the most strongly induced metabolites at cold temperature (Fig. 1a), we focused subsequent analyses on this metabolite. Cold exposure after development increased BH2 levels from day 1 of adulthood, and BH2 remained elevated at 15 °C relative to age-matched animals at 20 °C until at least day 10 (Fig. 1c and Supplementary Table 3). BH2 induction was not a general consequence of longevity, as reduced insulin signalling, a distinct longevity pathway, did not increase BH2 levels at 20 °C (Extended Data Fig. 1e and Supplementary Table 4).

Loss of the temperature-sensitive trpa-1 channel shortens lifespan under both standard and cold temperatures11. However, worms still exhibit substantially extended lifespan at 15 °C compared with at 20 °C, indicating that cold-induced longevity is not solely mediated by TRPA-1-dependent pathways. Loss of trpa-1 did not reduce BH2 levels at cold temperature (Fig. 1d and Supplementary Table 1), demonstrating that elevated BH2 synthesis is induced independently of canonical TRPA-1 temperature sensing.

We next examined whether BH2 reflects a regulated pathway rather than a passive metabolic consequence of reduced temperature. Quantitative proteomics identified GTP cyclohydrolase I (GCH1 in humans, CAT-4 in C. elegans), the first and rate-limiting enzyme in BH2 synthesis16,17, as one of the most strongly induced proteins at 15 °C (Fig. 1e and Supplementary Table 5). Cold temperature also increased cat-4 mRNA amounts, indicating that CAT-4 induction occurs, at least in part, at the transcriptional level (Extended Data Fig. 2a). MED15 (human)/MDT-15 (C. elegans), a key transcriptional regulator of metabolic responses, is required for cold-induced longevity9. Importantly, knockdown of mdt-15 reduced cat-4 transcript levels at 15 °C (Extended Data Fig. 2b), indicating that cold-induced CAT-4 expression is part of a regulated transcriptional program.

Although BH2 is an inactive metabolite, it functions as a key intermediate in the synthesis and intertissue transport of its reduced, bioactive form, BH416,17. As BH4 is chemically unstable and readily oxidized, the more-stable BH2 form serves as a major organismal reservoir of this bioactive metabolite16,17. Consequently, cat-4-mutant worms are severely depleted of BH418. Consistent with the essential role of BH4 as a cofactor for multiple enzymes, these worms exhibit increased fragility compared with wild-type worms18. Accordingly, cat-4-mutant worms were short lived under both cold and standard temperatures (Extended Data Fig. 2c–e). Knockdown of cat-4 from early development also shortened lifespan at both 15 °C and 20 °C, although the effect was more pronounced under cold temperatures (Extended Data Fig. 2f).

As cold temperature during adulthood was sufficient to induce CAT-4 expression, BH2 synthesis and lifespan extension, we next examined the effects of cat-4 knockdown after development. As BH4 could not be detected by metabolomics because of its chemical instability, we quantified BH4 using an enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay (ELISA). Cold temperature induced a significant but modest increase in BH4 levels (Fig. 1f), consistent with BH2 serving as the major intermediate reservoir for this metabolite16,17. Knockdown of cat-4 after development decreased BH4 levels under both standard and cold temperatures, although this reduction was moderate (Fig. 1f). Importantly, cat-4 RNA interference (RNAi) decreased BH4 at cold temperature only to levels comparable to those observed at standard temperature without knockdown (Fig. 1f). Thus, BH4 levels remained within a physiological range, enabling us to determine whether elevated CAT-4 activity during adulthood is required for cold-induced longevity. Notably, knockdown of cat-4 after development diminished the lifespan extension induced by cold temperature, but had no significant effect on lifespan at 20 °C or 25 °C (Fig. 1g).

To further dissect the adult-specific requirement for elevated CAT-4 in cold-induced longevity, we generated an auxin-inducible degron (AID) strain enabling conditional degradation of CAT-4 protein in somatic tissues (Extended Data Fig. 2g). CAT-4 degradation from early development substantially reduced lifespan at 20 °C (Extended Data Fig. 2h). By contrast, degradation of CAT-4 after development did not affect lifespan at 20 °C, but specifically shortened longevity at 15 °C (Extended Data Fig. 2i). These results further indicate that CAT-4 activity is essential during development for adult viability, whereas increased CAT-4 activity during adulthood specifically contributes to cold-induced longevity.

Prompted by these findings, we next examined whether CAT-4 activity also contributes to lifespan extension in other longevity paradigms. Knockdown of cat-4 in mutant worms with reduced insulin signalling did not affect their extended lifespan at standard temperature, but only shortened lifespan at 15 °C (Extended Data Fig. 3a,b). By contrast, knockdown of cat-4 shortened lifespan in long-lived worms with reduced mitochondrial electron-transport chain activity or reduced food intake at both cold and standard temperatures (Extended Data Fig. 3c–h), suggesting that CAT-4 activity contributes to other longevity paradigms beyond cold temperature.

Downstream of activity of GCH1/CAT-4, bioactive BH4 can be generated through three pathways: the de novo, salvage and recycling pathways16,17 (Fig. 1b). In the de novo pathway, a GCH1/CAT-4-dependent intermediate is converted to BH4 by sepiapterin reductase. In the salvage pathway, this intermediate is instead converted to BH2 by either aldose reductase (AKR1B1 (human), Y39G8B.1 (C. elegans)) or carbonyl reductase 1 (CBR1 (human), DHS-31 (C. elegans))16,19. This pathway operates alongside de novo BH4 synthesis, but is particularly important in mammalian tissues with low sepiapterin reductase activity and in organisms such as C. elegans, which lack a sepiapterin reductase orthologue16,18. Knockdown of the BH2-producing enzyme Y39G8B.1 reduced lifespan specifically at 15 °C, whereas loss of dhs-31 produced a smaller, non-significant effect (Extended Data Fig. 4a). In the salvage pathway, BH2 is further converted into bioactive BH4 by dihydrofolate reductase (DHFR (human), DHFR-1 (C. elegans)). Importantly, knockdown of dhfr-1 after development decreased longevity at cold temperature but did not shorten lifespan at standard temperature (Fig. 1h). These results indicate that, downstream of CAT-4 activity, the conversion of BH2 into bioactive BH4 by DHFR-1 contributes to cold-induced longevity.

A recycling mechanism regenerates BH4 after its use as an enzymatic cofactor16,18 (Fig. 1b). In C. elegans, the recycling pathway is not required to maintain steady-state BH4 levels and becomes rate-limiting only under conditions of low BH4 synthesis, such as in reduction-of-function cat-4 mutants18. Subsequently, knockdown of recycling enzymes did not shorten the lifespan of wild-type worms at cold temperature (Extended Data Fig. 4b). Likewise, mutant worms defective in BH4 recycling were not short lived at 15 °C compared with wild-type worms (Extended Data Fig. 4c,d), indicating that this pathway is dispensable for cold-induced longevity. Together, our findings support a central role of CAT-4 activity and the salvage pathway in elevated BH4 synthesis for cold-induced longevity.

To determine the tissues in which CAT-4 is expressed, we generated a GFP-based fluorescent reporter driven by the endogenous cat-4 promoter. Knockdown of cat-4 reduced the GFP signal, validating the reporter (Extended Data Fig. 5a). At standard temperature, CAT-4 was mostly expressed in the anterior intestine, and cold exposure further increased CAT-4 levels in this region (Extended Data Fig. 5a,b). In contrast to the intestine, CAT-4 was barely detectable in muscle at 20 °C (Fig. 2a). However, cold temperature triggered robust expression of cat-4 in the muscle, an effect that was abolished by cat-4 RNAi treatment (Fig. 2a,b). To assess whether elevated BH2 synthesis in these tissues promotes organismal longevity at cold temperature, we performed tissue-specific knockdown experiments. Notably, knockdown of cat-4 in the muscle alone reduced cold-induced longevity, whereas knockdown in the intestine had no effect on lifespan (Fig. 2c,d). Likewise, knockdown of cat-4 in neurons, epidermis or germline did not shorten lifespan at 15 °C (Fig. 2e–g).

a, Representative images of day 5 adult worms expressing cat-4p::cat-4::SL2::GFP at the indicated temperatures. Cold temperature induces cat-4 expression in muscle cells. b, cat-4 RNAi after development abolishes cold-induced CAT-4 upregulation in muscle. In a,b, the arrows indicate CAT-4 in muscle. Scale bars, 20 µm (a,b). Images are representative of three independent experiments. c–g, Tissue-specific cat-4 knockdown (KD) after development. Muscle-specific knockdown shortens cold-induced longevity (c; P < 0.0001), whereas intestinal (d; P = 0.5374), neuronal (e; P = 0.8708), epidermal (f; P = 0.8339) or germline (g; P = 0.2604) knockdown does not. h, Single knockdown of ent-1 (P < 0.0001), ent-5 (P < 0.0001) or ent-6 (P < 0.0001), but not ent-4 (P = 0.9091), shortens lifespan at 15 °C. i, Knockdown of ent-1 (P = 0.2209), ent-5 (P = 0.9840) or ent-6 (P = 0.1278) does not shorten lifespan at 20 °C. j–n, Tissue-specific dhfr-1 RNAi after development. Muscle (j; P = 0.1076) or epidermal (k; P = 0.1830) knockdown does not affect cold-induced longevity, whereas neuronal (l; P < 0.0001), intestinal (m; P = 0.0001) or germline (n; P = 0.0001) knockdown reduces lifespan at 15 °C. In c–n, n = 96 worms per condition; data are representative of at least two independent experiments. Statistical comparisons were performed using two-sided log-rank tests. Data are mean ± s.e.m. Details of statistical analyses, and replicate data of lifespan experiments are provided in Supplementary Table 7.

To further investigate the link between cold-induced CAT-4 expression in muscle and longevity, we generated worms in which auxin-inducible degradation of CAT-4 was restricted to muscle. We confirmed that auxin treatment specifically depleted CAT-4 in the muscle of these animals, whereas ubiquitous somatic degradation reduced CAT-4 levels in both muscle and intestine (Extended Data Fig. 5c,d). Notably, muscle-specific degradation of CAT-4 after development was sufficient to fully phenocopy the longevity-shortening effects of ubiquitous somatic CAT-4 degradation at 15 °C (Extended Data Fig. 5e). This effect was specific to cold temperature, as muscle-specific CAT-4 degradation did not affect lifespan at standard temperature (Extended Data Fig. 5f). Together, these results identify muscle as a critical tissue in which elevated BH2 synthesis promotes cold-induced longevity.

BH4 is essential for the function of amino acid hydroxylases (TPH1 (human), TPH-1 (C. elegans); TH (human), CAT-2 (C. elegans); PAH (human), PAH-1 (C. elegans) and alkylglycerol monooxygenase (AGMO (human), AGMO-1 (C. elegans)). However, these enzymes are not expressed in muscle cells18, raising the possibility that BH4 acts in a cell non-autonomous manner. BH2 is the principal transport intermediate for BH4 distribution between tissues, enabling the rapid generation and utilization of bioactive BH4 in distal tissues16,17. We therefore hypothesized that cold temperature increases BH2 synthesis in muscle cells, which is then released and transported to distal tissues, where it is converted into BH4 to activate its target enzymes. In humans, the uptake of BH2 is mediated by the transmembrane nucleoside transporters ENT1 (SLC29A1) and ENT2 (SLC29A2)16. C. elegans expresses seven orthologues of human ENT1 and ENT2 (ent-1 to ent-7). We found that single knockdown of different orthologues, such as ent-1, ent-5 and ent-6, specifically shortens lifespan at 15 °C but not 20 °C (Fig. 2h,i).

Once BH2 is taken up by distal cells, it can be converted into bioactive BH4 through the salvage pathway by the DHFR-1 enzyme16,20. Consequently, BH4 redistribution efficiency depends on DHFR-1 activity, which regulates local BH4 availability. While BH2 synthesis through CAT-4 activity in muscle cells is required for cold-induced longevity (Fig. 2c), we found that muscle-specific knockdown of dhfr-1 does not shorten lifespan at 15 °C (Fig. 2j). Similarly, dhfr-1 knockdown in the epidermis had no effect on longevity at 15 °C (Fig. 2k). By contrast, tissue-specific knockdown of dhfr-1 in neurons, intestine and germline reduced cold-induced longevity (Fig. 2l–n). Moreover, neither neuron-specific knockdown of dhfr-1 nor muscle-specific knockdown of cat-4 further reduced the shortened lifespan of ent-1 mutant animals at cold temperature (Extended Data Fig. 5g,h). These results support the notion that cold temperature induces BH2 synthesis in muscle cells, after which, BH2 is released and converted to bioactive BH4 in distal tissues such as neurons, intestine and germline, where it promotes cold-induced longevity.

To further examine the cell non-autonomous effects of BH2, we focused on the intestine, a tissue that progressively loses structural integrity during ageing21,22. To this end, we assessed the intermediate filament IFB-2 because this protein is expressed exclusively in the intestine and undergoes age-related changes that can be readily monitored22. In young animals, IFB-2 is confined to the apical cytoplasm of intestinal cells. With age, IFB-2 levels increase and the protein becomes mislocalized throughout the cytoplasm, where it forms aggregates that contribute to intestinal decline22. We found that cold temperature prevents IFB-2 mislocalization and aggregation during ageing (Extended Data Fig. 6a). However, loss of cat-4 abolished these protective effects (Extended Data Fig. 6a). Notably, muscle-specific knockdown of cat-4 was sufficient to block the cold-induced suppression of intestinal IFB-2 aggregation (Extended Data Fig. 6b). Collectively, these findings indicate that cold-induced BH2 synthesis in muscle exerts cell non-autonomous effects, such as preserving intestinal integrity during ageing.

BH2 supplementation is often more effective at increasing BH4 levels than direct BH4 administration16,20. However, C. elegans exhibits a limited capacity for biopterin uptake, including BH2, and exogenous supplementation results in only a modest increase in biopterin levels18. Despite this limitation, BH2 supplementation in adult worms led to a slight but significant extension of cold-induced longevity (Extended Data Fig. 7a). Although BH2 supplementation did not extend lifespan at standard temperature, it delayed the age-related decline in motility, a key indicator of healthspan (Extended Data Fig. 7b,c). Although BH2-treated worms did not display overt signs of sickness, we further assessed the possibility that these beneficial effects arise from a hormetic stress response, such as that induced by mitochondrial dysfunction23,24. However, BH2 supplementation did not reduce brood size and did not induce the mitochondrial unfolded protein response (Extended Data Fig. 7d,e), two commonly used indicators of physiological stress.

Given the limited uptake of exogenous biopterins in worms, we used a genetic approach to assess the full potential of these metabolites to extend longevity. Ubiquitous overexpression of cat-4 in somatic tissues resulted in a substantial increase in cold-induced longevity (Extended Data Fig. 7f). Notably, overexpression of cat-4 in the muscle alone was sufficient to increase organismal levels of bioactive BH4 and extend lifespan not only at 15 °C, but also at 20 °C and 25 °C (Fig. 3a,b and Extended Data Fig. 7g–i). Moreover, muscle-specific cat-4 overexpression also extended lifespan in loss-of-function trpa-1 mutant worms across different temperatures (Extended Data Fig. 7j–l). These findings indicate that increasing CAT-4 activity in muscle promotes longevity independently of both temperature and the cold-sensitive channel TRPA-1.

a,b, Muscle-specific cat-4 overexpression (OE) extends lifespan at 15 °C and 20 °C (a; P < 0.0001) and at 25 °C (b; P < 0.0001). c, Filter-trap analysis of intestinal IFB-2 aggregation (detected by anti-IFB-2 antibody) in control and muscle-specific cat-4-overexpressing worms at 20 °C on days 1 and 10 of adulthood. Right, SDS–PAGE and immunoblotting of IFB-2 and the α-tubulin loading control. Images are representative of three independent experiments; quantification is shown in Extended Data Fig. 7m. d, Representative images of intestinal colonization by mCherry-expressing Escherichia coli in control and muscle-specific cat-4-overexpressing worms on days 1 and 10. The box plots represent the 25th–75th percentiles, the lines depict the median and the whiskers show the minimum–maximum values.
