#3275. Drained Under Soviet Rule, Aral Sea Has Become a Huge Source of Emissions

This article was originally published by Grist and is reproduced here as part of the Climate Desk collaboration.

Abandoned boasts in dried-up Aral Sea. Arian Zwegers via Flickr

The Aral Sea sits between Kazakhstan and Uzbekistan and was once the fourth-largest inland body of water on Earth. For the past 60 years, though, humans have bled it nearly dry irrigating cotton crops, leaving behind a salty plain the size of Ireland. Its loss has long been seen as an ecological and humanitarian problem, but new research shows that it has also been a significant driver of climate change.

The Aral Sea is technically a lake. But when nearly any body of water is full and works as it should, organic matter collects on the bottom, where it remains trapped, often for centuries or millennia. “They accumulate carbon in the sediment,” explained Rafael Marcé, a research scientist at the Centre for Advanced Studies in Blanes, Spain and the lead author of the study, which was published Thursday in the journal Science. “They are carbon sinks.”

If the water dries up, however, stored carbon is released, turning sinks into sources. It’s something that Marcé has seen in smaller lakes he’s studied, but even he was surprised by what his team found during its 2022 expedition to Central Asia. “We didn’t go to the Aral Sea blind. We had some previous evidence,” he said. “We had no idea about the potential magnitude.”

The Aral Sea has left behind a timeline of sorts. The edges dried out decades back, while some areas were wet until just a few years ago. Marcé and his colleagues collected samples along this gradation. The technique allowed them to reconstruct how much carbon the lake had emitted as it evaporated. The figure is staggering. Between 1960 and 2022, they found, the Aral Sea had pumped a remarkable 748 million metric tons of carbon dioxide into the atmosphere. That’s three times the annual emissions of Spain.

“At the beginning it goes pretty fast, then it slowly decays,” said Marcé, noting that about half of the carbon dioxide is released in the first 15 years after a section of the lake is exposed. The paper also found that nearly a fifth of emissions came from wind blowing sediment away, an aspect of drying that experts say hadn’t been adequately studied before.

“This dust is a really big issue,” said Sarian Kosten, an aquatic ecologist and professor at Radboud University who was not involved in this research. She called the overall science fascinating and sound, yet the trend disheartening. “I always find it very sad to see these pictures of the declining water surface there.”

Satellite images of the Aral Sea in 1987 (left) 2024 (right). U.S. Geological Survey

One of the study’s limitations, said Marcé, is that scientists could bring only relatively light-duty equipment to the Aral Sea. That meant that their sediment cores were capped at 50 centimeters — about 20 inches — even though the lake bed is many feet thick. The researchers aren’t sure what is happening further down. “It could be that all these degradation effects are contained in the first layers,” he said. Or, “all these carbon calculations we did could be a gross underestimate.”

The group plans to return with bigger drills next year to learn more. The paper also highlights the many other places around the world that are in the midst of this “dry flux” phenomenon. That includes Lake Chad in western Africa, Bolivia’s Lake Poopó, and the Caspian Sea, which is the world’s largest inland body of water and is expected to shrink by more than the entire area of the Aral Sea by the end of the century. The Salton Sea in California is also mentioned, as is Utah’s Great Salt Lake, which another recent study found is releasing over 4 million tons of carbon dioxide into the atmosphere each year.

“There is a whole bunch of CO2 coming out of the ground that no one was counting,” said Soren Brothers, the author of the Great Salt Lake paper, a limnologist at the University of Toronto, and the climate curator at the Royal Ontario Museum in Canada. But researchers are increasingly putting numbers to that “huge blind spot” and he’s impressed by the latest Aral Sea study, which he wasn’t involved in. “This is adding to the story of these inland waters.”

The mounting evidence around these emissions is particularly worrying because it could be a sign of a climate tipping point from which it would become difficult, if not impossible, to recover. “If we keep on doing this kind of stuff, where we are drying up lakes,” said Brothers, ”those could take over driving climate change.”

The Aral Sea paper also examined how much carbon dioxide has yet to be released from the lake, and pegged the number at about 605 million metric tons. While that makes the salt flat a ticking climate bomb, Marcé and his coauthors argue that this also means there’s an opportunity to reverse course. “We want to spotlight the fact that we have all this carbon that can be protected,” said Marcé. “It’s offering a solution. Or a conversation at least.”

Keeping that amount of carbon dioxide in the ground would be equivalent to about $18 billion worth of carbon credits, the paper estimates. Brothers thinks this attention to potential paths forward is among the most interesting parts of the paper. “That’s a new contribution,” said Brothers, and the logic could apply to any body of water that’s storing carbon, from reservoirs like Lake Mead on the Colorado River to urban ponds.

”I see all of this shaping into a conversation of how do we improve things,” he said, adding that there has been plenty of research on the human, climate, and economic harms of letting lakes dry out. “We need to start researching what is the path forward.”

Marcé acknowledges that there are no easy answers for the Aral Sea. The problem began in the 1960s when the Soviet Union diverted water from the rivers that feed it for cotton-crops. Many of those irrigation systems remain outdated, and improving them would save water. Even then, though, getting the water back in the sea would require convincing multiple jurisdictions not to just reuse it for other purposes. Still, fixes like this aren’t impossible, said Marcé, and linking the issue to carbon credits could provide incentives to find them.

“If we have this kind of program, there could be a hope for the Aral Sea,” he said. “It’s at least a chance.”

A Missing Piece in Climate Models: Nature’s Own Emissions

#3276. Apple AirPods 5 review: the best open-fit earbuds yet

Guardian journalism is independent. We earn a commission if you buy something through an affiliate link. Learn more.

Upgraded, cheaper AirPods have noise cancelling, sound and controls to rival many plugged-in buds but without tips

Apple’s latest AirPods set a new standard for open-fit buds with upgraded noise cancelling that now rivals many plugged-in designs of the same price.

The AirPods 5 have the same design the popular AirPods 4 offering an option for those that do not like silicone ear tips used by most noise cancelling earbuds. They come in two versions, with or without a wireless charging case and swipe controls, costing from £119 (€149/$129/A$219) – £10 cheaper than the launch price of their predecessors.

The earbuds and case are almost indistinguishable from the AirPods 4, which makes the case very small and easy to pocket, fitting in the watch pocket of a pair of jeans.

The buds are some of the most comfortable open-fit designs available and stayed put when commuting, running for the train and generally going about the day. But for those that didn’t get on with the previous design of the AirPods, these are unlikely to change that, and they have no stability wings or similar to keep them in place for vigorous exercise.

The stalk has a pressure-sensitive panel you squeeze once, twice or thrice for playback controls. A squeeze and hold changes the noise cancelling mode or activates Siri. On the more expensive versions with a wireless charging case, the panel is also touch-sensitive for swipe volume controls – the same system used in the AirPods Pro to great effect.

Connectivity: Bluetooth 5.3, SBC, AAC, H2 chip

Battery life: four or five hours with ANC (22 hours with case)

Water resistance: IP57 (up to 1 metre for 30 minutes)

Charging case dimensions: 46.2 x 50.1 x 21.2mm

Case charging: USB-C or wireless (Qi or Watch)

The cheapest AirPods 5 have up to four hours playback with noise cancelling and a case that can charge them four times for a total of 20 hours playback.

The AirPods 5 with wireless charging case have longer battery life lasting slightly more than five hours of playback with noise cancelling with a total of 22 hours playback using charges in the case. That’s long enough for a week of commuting, but far behind the best in the business. A compromise for the design and super-compact case.

The new AirPods have noticeably improved noise cancelling and sound. They produce fairly detailed sound with good separation of tones and can handle complex and overlapping tracks very well. But it is the additional bass that helps them produce a fuller, more rounded sound that wipes the floor with most other open-fit rivals and compares well to many decent earbuds with silicone tips. They may not satisfy audiophiles but they are a great listen.

The noise cancelling is equally effective, managing to quiet busy roads, the drone of engines or fans, and dampen background chatter. It struggles with higher tones, such as loud keyboard clicks or the screeches from train wheels, but does as good a job as many similarly-priced noise cancelling earbuds with silicone tips and is miles ahead of the open-fit competition.

They suffer a little from wind noise and can’t rival the sound dampening of the best earbuds with tips from Sony, Bose or the AirPods Pro 3, but still do a solid job.

The transparency mode is first class, sounding almost exactly like not wearing them at all, which is great for awareness on the street. Call quality is equally good coming through loud and clear with only a hint of sudden background noise when passing something particularly loud.

The AirPods 5 support Apple’s various smart features with an iPhone, such as direct access to the new Siri, live translation, automatic volume adjustment, spatial audio, audio sharing, the ability to pause music when falling asleep and optimised battery charging to increase its longevity.

Batteries in earbuds typically maintain at least 80% of their original capacity for 500 charge cycles. Apple offers a battery service for £49 per earbud or case and replacements cost from £69. The specialists iFixit awarded the earbuds two out of 10 for repairability. The AirPods 5 are made with 40% recycled material, which the company breaks down in its environmental impact report. The company offers trade-in and free recycling schemes, including for non-Apple products.

The AirPods 5 cost £119 (€149/$129/A$219) or £139 (€169/$149/A$249) with a wireless charging case and touch controls.

For comparison, the AirPods Pro 3 cost £219, the AirPods Max 2 cost £499, the Google Pixel Buds 2a cost £129 and the Shokz OpenFit Air 2 cost £129.

The AirPods 5 take the comfortable open-fit design of their predecessors and upgrade the sound and noise cancelling to rival many plugged-in earbuds with silicone tips, which is quite a feat.

They provide a good amount of bass for a full, detailed sound that’s a great general listen and a step above most open-fit rivals. The noise cancelling is capable of dampening unwanted sound to tolerable levels, comparing well with better than average silicone-equipped earbuds. It can’t match the best plugged-in earbuds, but it is certainly good enough. Apple’s transparency mode and call quality are top-notch, and it’s hard to fault the earbuds for daily use with a compact design that’s easy to pocket.

The battery life is a little short, and while they work with any Bluetooth device, I wouldn’t recommend them to people who don’t have at least one iPhone, iPad or Mac.

There are few great open-fit earbuds, but if you didn’t get on with other AirPods, these won’t tip the balance. The extra money for swipe volume controls and longer battery life is worth it, if you’re considering them.

The fifth-generation earbuds are marginally more repairable than their predecessors, which opens up the possibility of replacing the batteries down the line, but otherwise they are just as disposable as most other earbuds.

Pros: comfortable open fit, good sound and noise cancelling, great case, great playback and volume controls, great connectivity, special features with Apple devices including spatial audio, great call quality.

Cons: battery life could be better, little passive noise isolation, lack of features when connected to non-Apple devices, no Bluetooth multipoint or higher quality audio format support, difficult to repair.

#3277. What is a Mast Year

Have you noticed more acorns than usual this year? It feels like everywhere we go, there are paths filled with acorns and leaves.

A mast year occurs when trees and shrubs produce more seeds than usual. The collective term for fruits, nuts and seeds produced by trees is called mast, hence the term mast year. A mast year occurs every 2-5 years.

Mast years don’t just happen for individual trees. It is usual for most trees of a particular species to have a mast year at the same time. Scientists don’t know how this synchronisation happens, but they do know that the weather plays a role. To have a bumper crop one year, the temperature and rainfall have to support the growth.

One theory as to why mast years happen is predator satiation. When smaller crops happen over a period of a few years, there’s enough food for the animals that depend on them, but not enough for population growth, so the number of predators remains low. When a mast year occurs, there’s more food than the low population of predators can eat, which means that a lot of the seeds are left uneaten and can germinate and grow into new trees. The huge number of seeds also leads to a boost in the population of the mammals that depend on them,

For a tree to produce such a huge quantity of seeds takes a lot of energy, which is why they cannot do it every year.

Find out how tall a tree is using a clever maths trick.

Discover why leaves change colour in autumn.

#3280. Five key tax talking points before next week’s budget

As the chancellor prepares for 28 October, we unpack the facts and figures – and the implications any changes could mean for you

As John Healey prepares his first budget as chancellor, tax is the small word that is likely to cast a large shadow over next week’s big fiscal announcement.

In its election manifesto, Labour promised not to increase the “big three”: income tax, national insurance and VAT.

As a result, much of the pre-budget speculation has centred on which other levies Healey might seek to change.

No one seems to like paying tax, but some taxes – or the rules around them – are more unpopular than others. Here, we look at some that have been under the spotlight recently, and the facts and figures behind them.

Inheritance tax: is it a levy on middle-class families?

In a recent sales pitch to voters, the Conservative leader Kemi Badenoch pledged to “abolish inheritance tax [IHT] as soon as we can afford it”.

She said IHT was “supposed to be a tax on the super-wealthy” but had become a “tax on ordinary middle-class families in every corner of our country”.

It is true that the sums collected through the tax have reached record levels. A total of £8.5bn was paid in the 2025-26 tax year – up from £8.2bn the year before.

The government’s forecaster, the Office for Budget Responsibility (OBR), predicts £9.5bn will be raised through IHT this year and £14.5bn by 2030-31.

At present, about 5% of estates pay IHT – roughly 30,000 a year. However, rising house prices and frozen allowances, plus recent rule changes that will pull some people’s pensions into the net, mean IHT has become of growing concern among some families who do not consider themselves to be wealthy.

The standard IHT rate is 40%, and could be payable on assets above the £325,000 tax-free allowance everyone is entitled to. However, beyond this headline rate are allowances that remove or reduce the bill for most families.

This tax-free threshold is increased to £500,000 by an additional £175,000 residence allowance if you leave your main home to a direct descendant (for estates worth less than £2m).

You can leave everything to your spouse without paying tax, and they also inherit any unused allowances. This means a couple can leave up to £1m to children or grandchildren without attracting the tax.

Government figures show that these allowances bring down the effective rate people pay. So across all of the estates paying IHT in 2023-24, the average effective tax rate was 13%.

On estates worth between £1m and £1.5m, where there was IHT to pay, the average bill was £151,000, or 12% of the total value.

A rule change introduced by Rachel Reeves when she was chancellor means, that from April 2027, any money left unspent in a defined contribution (AKA money purchase) pension after your death, will be included in the calculation for the first time.

The government estimates that, of about 213,000 estates with inheritable pension wealth in 2027-28, 10,500 will have to pay IHT where previously they would not.

Meanwhile, 38,500 estates will be landed with a bigger bill. The average IHT bill is expected to increase by about £34,000 when pension assets are included.

Changes to the rules have turned capital gains tax (CGT) into a big money-spinner for the government, with the amount raised surging nearly 80% in a year to reach £24.3bn in 2025-26.

It is a tax on the profit you make when you sell – or, to put it in HM Revenue and Customs language, “dispose of” – something that has increased in value such as a second home, or investments that are not held within an Isa. That sum of £24.3bn compared with £13.7bn the previous year.

You get an annual tax-free allowance, but it is much less generous than it was, thanks to two cuts by Jeremy Hunt, when he was the Conservative chancellor. Until 2022-23 it was £12,300, but it was reduced to £6,000, and then to £3,000.

Partly owing to this, the number of people who paid CGT in 2024-25 was up 45% on the previous year, hitting an “all-time high” of 584,000. (This compares with more than 34 million income taxpayers.)

However, according to HMRC, most CGT is paid by a small number of taxpayers.

In 2024-25, nearly half of the tax raised came from those who made gains of £5m or more. This group represents less than 1% of CGT taxpayers each year, it says.

Official data shows that CGT is most likely to be paid by those aged between 55 and 64. Alongside 45- to 54-year-olds, these two age groups represented 44% of those paying CGT and 56% of the tax paid in 2024-25.

The rate of CGT you pay when you have used up your allowance was increased by Reeves in her autumn 2024 budget to 18% for basic-rate taxpayers and 24% for higher or additional rate taxpayers.

Senior Labour figures have lobbied Healey to raise rates to the same level as those on income tax (20%, 40% and 45% for basic, higher and additional rate respectively). They argue earned income should not be taxed more heavily than gains from assets.

This week, it was being reported that Healey has rejected calls to raise CGT in the budget, though nothing is certain.

Those supporting higher rates can point to a country such as Denmark, where CGT on investment gains is up to 42%.

From April 2028, owners of properties in England valued at £2m or more, will have to pay the high-value council tax surcharge – commonly known as the “mansion tax” – on top of existing council tax.

There are four price bands, with the annual charge ratcheting up from £2,500 for properties valued between £2m and £2.5m to £7,500 for those worth £5m and above.

The government says “fewer than 1%” of residential properties in England will be affected by the surcharge, which the OBR estimates will raise £400m a year.

The estate agent Hamptons estimates there are just over 138,000 homes in England (mainly in London and the south-east) worth more than £2m.

In the government consultation about the new surcharge, it was suggested help would be at hand for people who live in valuable homes but do not have a big income. Under its proposals, this could mean people with a household income of £35,000 or less, and savings of £16,000 or less, would be able to defer the annual charge.

While Badenoch has pledged to scrap this tax, too, it was reported that Healey considered – but then rejected – a plan to extend it to properties worth £1.5m. This would have doubled the number of homes within the tax’s scope.

This is one of the biggest outgoings for many households, and hundreds of thousands of Londoners have been told to brace for large bill increases next year.

Hammersmith and Fulham has warned residents their bills could rise by 150% to maintain services, while neighbouring Wandsworth and Westminster are considering a near-doubling of bills.

For Hammersmith and Fulham residents, a 150% rise would increase the annual council tax bill of a household living in a typical band D property to £2,522.50, up from £1,009.

These councils are among six in England permitted to raise bills by an unlimited amount over the next two years. Five are in London (the others are Kensington and Chelsea and City of London), and the other is Windsor and Maidenhead in Berkshire.

These councils have big holes in their budgets because the money they receive from central government has been dramatically reduced under the Fair Funding formula. This gives higher weighting (and thus a greater relative share of overall resources) to local authorities with high “deprivation” scores.

While proposed bill increases will be a blow to struggling households, Stuart Hoddinott, an associate director at the Institute for Government thinktank, says councils involved have received higher grant funding relative to need for a long time and used it to subsidise low council tax rates.

This year, a band D household bill in Wandsworth is £1,028, while in next door Lambeth the equivalent rate is £2,047. Meanwhile, residents of Kensington and Chelsea are paying £1,667 for band D homes, while in Blackpool they are stumping up £2,513.

“From an individual perspective, these bills will be a shock,” says Hoddinott, who, as a Hammersmith resident, is personally affected. However, he adds that these local authorities currently have among the lowest rates in England.

“Bills in Westminster and Wandsworth are about 43% of the average rate in the country, which means that even if they double, they would still be in the bottom half of councils for council tax. They’re exceptionally low.”

Several million more pensioners have started paying income tax since the personal allowance was frozen at £12,570 five years ago. In 2022-23, it was 7.1 million and this year the number is 9.6 million.

Typically, pensioners whose only income is the state pension do not pay tax because it adds up to less than the tax-free allowance. Those who do pay tax are usually those receiving money from a private or workplace pension.

However, when the full new state pension is increased next April, thanks to the “triple lock” it will top £13,000 a year – creating a headache for the government.

In total, about 13.3 million people claim the state pension, and the pensions minister, Torsten Bell, has said those who only just exceed the personal allowance will not need to pay tax. Bell has promised that the chancellor will spell out how this will work in practice in the budget.

It was all but confirmed this week that pensioners would get a 3.9% state pension rise next year. That will mean the annual full new state pension rising to £13,036.

Steve Webb, a former pensions minister who is now a partner at the consultancy LCP, suggests that the government will work out the tax bills for those whose sole income is the new state pension.

Based on the 3.9% increase next April, which would result in a £90 tax bill, Webb suggests they might not issue demands for anything under £100.

#3282. Opposition in Venezuela meldet sich zurück

Seit der völkerrechtlich umstrittenen Festnahme von Venezuelas Langzeitmachthaber Maduro ist dessen Stellvertreterin Rodríguez auf Kuschelkurs mit US-Präsident Trump. Jetzt begehren Regierungsgegner erneut auf.

In Venezuela haben Tausende Menschen auf einer Kundgebung die Rückkehr der Oppositionspolitikerin und Friedensnobelpreisträgerin María Corina Machado gefordert. In der Hauptstadt Caracas versammelten sich rund 2000 Demonstranten auf der Plaza Venezuela und hielten Plakate in die Höhe, auf denen sie die Abhaltung von Wahlen verlangten. Auch im Bundesstaat Táchira an der Grenze zu Kolumbien fand eine Kundgebung statt.

Es waren die größten Demonstrationen in dem südamerikanischen Land seit der Entmachtung von Staatschef Nicolás Maduro Anfang des Jahres durch die USA. Machado lebt im Exil in Panama. Sie hatte Venezuela vor einem Jahr heimlich verlassen, um in Norwegens Hauptstadt Oslo den Friedensnobelpreis entgegenzunehmen. Nach Angaben ihrer Partei Vente Venezuela wurde sie bereits mehrfach an einer Rückkehr in ihr Heimatland gehindert.

Vor gut einer Woche begannen ohne Machado Verhandlungen zwischen Vertretern der Regierung von Übergangspräsidentin Delcy Rodríguez und der Opposition. Dabei bekräftigten beide Seiten ihren Willen, Präsidentenwahlen in Venezuela abzuhalten. Das US-Außenministerium begrüßte die Gespräche. Diese legten die Grundlage für freie und glaubwürdige Wahlen, hieß aus Washington.

Nach einer von Fälschungsvorwürfen überschatteten Präsidentenwahl hatte sich der Linksnationalist Maduro im Sommer 2024 erneut zum Sieger erklärt. Der prominenten Regierungsgegnerin Machado war eine Kandidatur von den venezolanischen Behörden damals untersagt worden.

Es folgte eine Welle der Repression, bei der 28 Menschen getötet wurden. Zahlreiche Oppositionelle gingen ins Exil.

Das US-Militär hatte Maduro Anfang Januar in Caracas gefangengenommen und in die USA gebracht. Nun kommen er und seine Ehefrau Cilia Flores in New York vor Gericht. Ihnen soll wegen "Drogenterrorismus" und Foltervorwürfen im kommenden Jahr der Prozess gemacht werden.

US-Präsident Donald Trump hatte nach Maduros Sturz gesagt, Venezuela stehe nun unter der Kontrolle der Vereinigten Staaten. Nach Angaben der Menschenrechtsorganisation Foro Penal sind in dem südamerikanischen Land weiterhin mehr als 300 Menschen aus politischen Gründen inhaftiert.

#3283. In Steel Country, the Fight for Clean Air Faces New Obstacles

The Edgar Thomson Works steel mill looms over Braddock, Pennsylvania. Justin Merriman / Bloomberg via Getty Images

A Japanese steel giant has pledged billions in new investment in Pennsylvania’s Monongahela River Valley, raising hopes that it would clean up pollution from its steel mills in the region. But documents show that its plans to ramp up production will worsen local air quality.

As it runs north to Pittsburgh from its headwaters in West Virginia, the roughly 130-mile-long Monongahela River carves a winding path through a steep-walled basin dotted with small towns and forested hillsides. But the prettiness of this place, known to locals as the Mon Valley, belies a long history of pollution and a drawn-out battle for clean air.

In 1875, Andrew Carnegie opened his first steel mill along the river in Braddock, near Pittsburgh, launching more than a century of heavy industry. By 1901, Carnegie’s company had merged with others to become U.S. Steel. The Mon Valley was the epicenter of domestic steel production, and the air was thick with smog.

Between 1970, when the Clean Air Act was passed, and 2020, emissions of key air pollutants nationwide dropped by 78 percent. In the Mon Valley, however, improvement has been more halting. The American Lung Association’s 2026 State of the Air report ranked Allegheny County, which includes the Mon Valley, among the most polluted places in the nation, with failing grades for unhealthy ozone levels, spikes in exposure to fine particulate pollution, and chronic exposure to those fine particles throughout the year.

The public health impact is considerable: A study published in January in the Annals of Global Health found that in 2019 alone some 12 percent of adult deaths and more than 400 adverse birth outcomes in the greater Pittsburgh area were attributable to air pollution. Across Allegheny County, childhood asthma rates are about 11 percent, compared to a national average of 6.7 percent. But research led by Deborah Gentile, a pediatric allergist in Pittsburgh, found that for children living closest to the cluster of plants known as the Mon Valley Works, asthma rates were more than 22 percent.

Now, the valley may be at an inflection point. Federal industrial policy has directed funding toward manufacturing nationwide, and the recent acquisition of U.S. Steel by the Japanese company Nippon Steel comes with a promise of billions in investment in the region and plans for a new manufacturing facility. But while Nippon is moving to curb carbon emissions from its global operations by embracing cleaner steelmaking technologies, in the already polluted Mon Valley it is doubling down on the use of coal, erecting a new hot strip mill that will drive up emissions of several pollutants, documents show.

“People recognize that the steel company has often lapsed in its promises to reduce emissions or invest in facilities,” said Patrick Campbell, executive director of the Pittsburgh-based Group Against Smog and Pollution (GASP). “They’re right to be deeply skeptical about what’s actually being invested in here.”

The air pollution problems originate at the Mon Valley Works, whose three steel mills emit particulate matter as well as benzene, ammonia, the respiratory irritant sulfur dioxide, and hydrogen sulfide, a flammable gas with a strong rotten egg smell.

Monessen, Pennsylvania, a town on the Monongahela River and site of a coking plant. Andrew Lichtenstein / Corbis via Getty Images

The Edgar Thomson Steel Works, in Braddock, uses massive blast furnaces to heat raw elements into molten steel. As of the Environmental Protection Agency’s last compliance monitoring a year ago, the plant had violated the Clean Air Act for nine straight quarters.

Just upriver in Clairton, the Clairton Coke Works uses huge ovens called batteries to bake coal at high temperatures to produce a purified fuel called coke that is used to run blast furnaces. The process creates emissions of both particulate matter and noxious gases, which are directly responsible, Gentile said, for asthma and other health issues.

“There’s tons of data linking [air pollution] to early deaths in adults, not to mention all the respiratory diseases including lung cancer and even [chronic obstructive pulmonary disease],” she said. “It can cause heart failure, arrhythmias, strokes. It’s also linked now with neurologic problems.”

The Clairton plant, according to EPA data last recorded in July 2025, had violated the Clean Air Act during the preceding 12 quarters. According to Allegheny County Health Department data, the plant is the source of more than 72 percent of the entire state’s hydrogen sulfide emissions and almost 93 percent of the county’s. Between March 2022 and November 2023, hydrogen sulfide levels at the monitor closest to the coke works exceeded the state standard of 5 parts per billion 159 times. In an enforcement order, the county noted that more than half of those exceedances were high enough to be considered of “major severity.”

The effects of air pollution are intensified by the valley’s topography, explains Albert Presto, who researches pollutant emissions in western Pennsylvania and is the director of Carnegie Mellon’s Center for Atmospheric Particle Studies. The factories’ stacks, he said, don’t reach above the rocky walls that flank the river. The area is also prone to inversions, in which the valley floor cools rapidly while warm air above acts like a lid. “That cooler air can get stuck down near the ground,” Presto explained, trapping pollutants with it.

Over the past 20 years, Presto said, pollution controls and the closing of four batteries at the Clairton Coke Works have contributed to a slow but significant reduction in the overall amount of particulate matter, ozone, and especially sulfur dioxide being released by the Mon Valley steelmaking facilities. “Mon Valley facilities have achieved environmental compliance rates exceeding 99 percent,” a U.S. Steel spokesperson wrote in response to questions. But air quality monitors operated by Allegheny County still routinely record spikes and hourly readings of particulate matter, ozone, and sulfur dioxide that exceed federal safety thresholds.

In mid-2025, Nippon officially acquired U.S. Steel in a deal that was previously blocked by the Biden Administration over national security concerns before being approved by President Donald Trump. The Japanese steelmaking firm, which has previously committed to achieving carbon neutrality by 2050, pledged to invest $11 billion in U.S. Steel by the end of 2028, including some $2 billion the companies said would be aimed at the Mon Valley Works.

The Edgar Thomson Works on the Monongahela River in Braddock, Pennsylvania. Jeff Swensen / Getty Images

There was hope among Mon Valley residents and environmental groups that money would be spent on equipment and updates that could lower pollution. “There are ways to make metallurgical coke that aren’t quite as polluting as the method they use in the Clairton Coke Works,” Presto said, “and ways to operate with better control on the emissions.”

Leaking coke battery doors, lids, and charging ports can be replaced or upgraded to create tighter seals, Presto said. More effective capture systems can collect gases before they escape. Additional controls, including high-efficiency dust-collection systems called baghouses, could help reduce releases of particulate matter, as could upgraded scrubbers — systems that trap or neutralize pollutants before they exit the smokestacks.

The most dramatic reduction in pollution would come from replacing coke-dependent blast furnace steelmaking with newer technologies, such as direct reduction furnaces, which can use hydrogen to prepare iron ore for refinement while emitting only water vapor, and electric arc furnaces, which use significantly less energy to melt scrap metal into steel. GASP’s Campbell said deploying these technologies would be transformative for the Mon Valley because they completely eliminate the need to produce or burn coke.

U.S. Steel announced in April that it is investing more than a billion dollars in direct reduction at the company’s Big River Steel Works in Arkansas, where it is already operating electric arc furnaces. But in the Mon Valley, the company plans to construct a hot strip mill, which can turn steel slabs produced by the nearby Edgar Thomson plant into thin, coiled sheets for use in automotive parts, pipes, and other products. The new mill will replace an 87-year-old hot strip mill at the Irvin Works, located 10 miles upriver. A spokesperson for U.S. Steel said that the new mill is “expected to improve efficiency, expand product capabilities, [and] reduce emissions intensity.” But the mill will be powered by coke oven gas, made using coke from the Clairton Works.

Nippon has offered no official decommission date for the Irvin plant when publicly describing its plans. Nathan Mallory, a member of the North Braddock Borough Council, is concerned the two facilities may run simultaneously for some time “and we’ll have extra saturation of pollution.”

The installation permit application submitted by U.S. Steel to the Allegheny County Health Department projected that its emissions of the smallest particulates, PM 2.5, would decrease from the 25.4 tons per year emitted at Irvin to an estimated 22 tons per year at the new hot strip mill. But the permit application also shows that overall emissions of particulate matter, carbon monoxide, nitrogen oxides, VOCs, and sulfur dioxide are expected to rise by more than 40 percent.

Coal barges on the Monongahela River near the Clairton Coke Works in Clairton, Pennsylvania. Justin Merriman / Bloomberg via Getty Images

“By their own calculations, they’ll be emitting increased pollution with this new facility,” Gentile said. “We need to be weighing the health impacts. It’s not just about what something will bring in revenue-wise, but about the real cost.” Mon Valley residents, she added, will bear the brunt of worsening air pollution. “[This] is an environmental justice area, meaning that many of them are minority patients, people of color, with low socioeconomic status. They can’t afford to move away.”

For a long time, said Mallory, there was little pushback locally against the steel company, despite the dismal air quality. “Nobody wanted to stand up against the mill,” he said, “because everybody’s grandfather retired from there, or their dad or their husband. As soon as I start talking about pollution, [some people] think I’m wanting to shut the mill down.”

But Mallory said he’s seen a shift since the deal with Nippon. People who were previously unwilling to support any measures that might jeopardize U.S. Steel’s profitability, he said, seem more prepared to be critical of new, foreign ownership. “I think the merger gave us a better middle ground,” he said. “And now we know the mill has the money to be able to produce steel safely and make updates to equipment to keep employees safe and the community healthy.” Nippon and U.S. Steel, which reported revenues of $63.4 billion and $16.5 billion, respectively, for fiscal year 2025, have not provided specific information about how the billions in new investment might reduce pollution.

“Steelmakers around the world employ different production methods based on market needs, available infrastructure, and customer requirements,” a U.S. Steel spokesperson said in response to questions about whether cleaner steel technology would be installed in the Mon Valley. “Our focus is on ensuring Mon Valley Works remains a competitive producer of advanced steel products through investments that improve efficiency, product quality, and environmental performance. The planned modernization projects are intended to position the Mon Valley for decades of high-value steelmaking.”

Since 2022, U.S. Steel has been fined more than $11 million by the county health department and the EPA. A lawsuit following a fire at Clairton Coke Works on Christmas Eve in 2018 led to a settlement that included a $5 million penalty and $37 million in mandated improvements to pollution control equipment. Another incident at the Clairton plant — an explosion that killed two steelworkers last year — resulted in fines of $118,000 levied by the Occupational Safety and Health Administration. Fines have funded monitoring equipment, research, public education, and the distribution of hundreds of air purifiers to residents.

But public health experts say such efforts are hardly commensurate with the scope of the problem. As long as emissions continue spiking, Gentile said, families in the Mon Valley will remain at risk.

“If you have asthma and you’re exposed to a high enough level of sulfur dioxide, it can cause an immediate bronchospasm and trigger an acute asthma attack,” she said. “Overall, pollution rates may have improved, but [the plants] still have bad days. And if you’re a kid with asthma, one bad day is all it takes.”

Kate Morgan is a journalist based in Pennsylvania. Her work has appeared in The New York Times, Popular Science, National Geographic, and many other publications. More about Kate Morgan →

#3285. Jelly Petri Dish Bacteria

These jelly/jello petri dish bacteria snacks are a fun Halloween party treat or just an unusual way to learn about bacteria!

A petri dish is a shallow, round dish with a loose-fitting lid used to grow bacteria in a laboratory. Microbiologists use them for experiments and investigations. In a laboratory setting, a layer of agar (or another growth medium) is spread over the base of the petri dish. When the agar sets, bacteria and other microorganisms can be grown under controlled conditions.

This activity is not an experiment, and we’re not growing bacteria, it’s just a fun learning activity.

Prepare the unflavored gelatin according to the instructions on the packet. This represents the growth medium.

Allow the mixture to cool slightly, then pour a thin layer into each petri dish. Allow to set completely.

Make the coloured jelly, following the instructions. Use the different colours to add circles and patterns to the clear jelly, resembling bacteria.

Grow bacteria on potatoes – always dispose of them carefully afterwards.

Find out why it’s important to wash your hands before eating.

Discover how bacteria are different to viruses.

#3286. WEE1 opposes APC/CCDH1 activity to promote S phase entry

Thank you for visiting nature.com. You are using a browser version with limited support for CSS. To obtain the best experience, we recommend you use a more up to date browser (or turn off compatibility mode in Internet Explorer). In the meantime, to ensure continued support, we are displaying the site without styles and JavaScript.

Nature (2026) Cite this article

WEE1 phosphorylates and inhibits the activity of cyclin-dependent kinases to prevent mitotic entry and establish a G2 DNA damage checkpoint1,2,3. Consequently, WEE1 inhibitors have been developed to be synthetically lethal in TP53-mutated tumours that become reliant on this WEE1-mediated checkpoint, and such inhibitors are undergoing clinical trials. However, WEE1 inhibitors also have efficacy in TP53-wild-type tumours, and many trials have been terminated owing to high levels of toxic side effects, suggesting that WEE1 has unknown functions. Here we show that WEE1 promotes cell cycle re-entry from quiescence (G0) by opposing the activity of the E3 ubiquitin ligase, anaphase-promoting complex–cyclosome bound to CDH1 (APC/CCDH1). WEE1 phosphorylates CDH1 (FZR1) at key residues that mediate the interaction between CDH1 and APC/C. Cells with loss of function of WEE1 during G0/G1 have delayed S phase entry, impaired G1/S transition, abnormal S phase accumulation of cyclin-dependent kinase inhibitor p21Cip1/Kip1 and a p21-dependent G2 arrest. Reduced expression of APC/CCDH1 or p21 renders cells more sensitive to acute WEE1 inhibition, and both pathways are downregulated in cells with acquired WEE1 inhibitor resistance. These results reveal a mechanism of cell cycle control that has implications for how WEE1 inhibitors should be used in the clinic.

Cyclin-dependent kinases (CDKs) control passage through the eukaryotic cell cycle. WEE1 (ref. 4) is a tyrosine kinase that inhibits CDK1 by phosphorylating Tyr15 (refs. 1,2,3). Late in G2, CDC25 phosphatases dephosphorylate Tyr15, promoting mitotic entry1,5,6,7,8. This mechanism forms a G2 DNA damage checkpoint, which becomes vital in TP53-mutated cancer cells9. Thus, WEE1 inhibitors (WEE1i) have been developed to be synthetically lethal in TP53-mutated cancers and are being trialled across several indications10,11. However, toxic side effects of unknown origin11 have led to trial suspensions. WEE1 also inhibits CDK2, a key driver of G1/S progression and proliferative commitment. Consistently, non-phosphorylatable CDK2T14A;Y15F knock-in mutant mice and human colorectal cancer cells enter S phase faster when returning to the cycle from quiescence12,13. However, no direct cellular evidence of the impact of WEE1 on CDK2 is available14, and exogenous WEE1 expression in IMR90 human fibroblasts has no effect on G1/S timing15. Therefore, we sought to clarify the function of WEE1 in early cell cycle control. We demonstrate a role for WEE1 in suppressing the anaphase-promoting complex–cyclosome bound to CDH1 (APC/CCDH1), a key antagonist of G1/S progression, to promote timely cell cycle re-entry following release from quiescence. We identify CDH1 (FZR1) as a WEE1 substrate relevant to cell cycle control. Our findings shed light on the pleiotropic impact of WEE1i in cells.

To understand the potential roles of WEE1 in regulation of CDK2 activity during G1 and S phase entry, we constructed an influence diagram describing the known interactions of WEE1 with the G1/S regulatory network (Fig. 1a). WEE1 inhibits cyclin E–CDK2 and cyclin A–CDK2 (ref. 16). We converted this influence diagram to a series of ordinary differential equations and parametrized the model to reproduce the known dynamics of the G1/S regulatory network (Fig. 1b). We simulated the depletion of WEE1 levels to 50% of baseline and observed that CDK2 was activated earlier and S phase entry was faster than in the presence of 100% WEE1 activity.

a, Influence diagram of the G1/S regulatory network. b, Time-course simulation of the ordinary differential equation model demonstrating how reduction of WEE1 activity to 50% of WT levels predicts a faster increase in CDK2 activity and a shorter G1 phase. Cyclin Etotal refers to Tyr15-phosphorylated (inactive) and Tyr15-unphosphorylated (active) CDK2 combined (blue dashed curve), whereas cyclin E is Tyr15-unphosphorylated CDK2 only (active, blue solid curve). The same logic applies to cyclin A in green. The G1/S transition, marked with a grey dashed line, was determined by the time of APC/CCDH1 switch-off (red solid curve) and increase in cyclin A–CDK2. c, Length of G1 phase (left) and fraction of cells that did not enter S phase (right) after WEE1i treatment upon release from CDK4/6i-mediated G0/G1 arrest. d, Length of G1 phase (left) and fraction of cells that did not enter S phase (right) after WEE1 depletion by siRNA 6 h before release from CDK4/6i-mediated G0/G1 arrest. e, Length of G1 phase (left) and fraction of cells that did not enter S phase (right) after WEE1 degradation by addition of doxycycline (Dox) and IAA upon release from CDK4/6i-mediated G0/G1 arrest. In c–e, the experimental timeline is shown above the graphs; all experiments involved n = 2 biological replicates; the mean ± s.d. are plotted with black lines; and individual data points are shown in blue or grey, coloured by experimental repeat. Significance was determined by unpaired Student’s t-test; ****P < 0.0001, **P < 0.01.

To experimentally test whether we could observe accelerated S phase entry in cells when WEE1 activity was reduced, we used AZD1775, a WEE1i previously used in clinical trials (Extended Data Fig. 1a–c). To investigate the role of WEE1 in S phase entry while avoiding confounding effects from WEE1 perturbation during the preceding G2 phase, we synchronized hTert-RPE1 mRuby–PCNA cells in early G0/G1 phase with a CDK4–CDK6 inhibitor (CDK4/6i) (Extended Data Fig. 1d). Labelling of PCNA at the endogenous locus with mRuby generates a robust marker to define the G1/S transition (Methods). Subsequently, we washed out the CDK4/6i, releasing cells into G1, in the presence or absence of WEE1i and quantified G1 length by time-lapse imaging of mRuby-labelled PCNA (Extended Data Fig. 1e). Contrary to our model predictions, WEE1 inhibition increased both G1 length and the fraction of cells that did not enter S phase during the 48 h imaging period (Fig. 1c). To confirm that this was not an artefact of CDK4/6i treatment, we also arrested cells in G0 by contact inhibition, followed by serum starvation (Extended Data Fig. 1f), then replated cells at low density, releasing the cells into G1 in the presence or absence of WEE1i, and again performed time-lapse imaging. Again, we observed increased G1 length and an increased fraction of cells not entering S phase after WEE1 inhibition (Extended Data Fig. 1g).

As kinase inhibitors can have off-target effects, we depleted WEE1 from CDK4/6i-arrested G0/G1 cells using short interfering RNA (siRNA) (Extended Data Fig. 1h), then released the cells back into the cell cycle and quantified G1 length by time-lapse imaging. WEE1 depletion had a more severe impact than WEE1 inhibition, with very few cells entering S phase over the 48 h imaging period compared with control-depleted cells and those that did so having an increased G1 length (Fig. 1d). To be certain that the delay in entering S phase was due to loss of WEE1 function, we generated a cell line with an inducible WEE1–GFP degron (Extended Data Fig. 2) to remove endogenous WEE1 in a highly specific manner. We validated WEE1–GFP localization and normal growth of this cell line and showed that WEE1 could be degraded within 1 h of addition of doxycycline and indole-3-acetic acid (IAA) (Extended Data Fig. 2b,d,e). Degradation of endogenous WEE1 upon release from CDK4/6i-mediated G0/G1 arrest again led to most cells not entering S phase; of those that did enter S phase, the length of G1 was significantly longer in the absence of WEE1 (Fig. 1e).

These data show that, contrary to our predictions and previous understanding of the functions of WEE1, WEE1 inhibition does not accelerate S phase entry. Instead, WEE1 is required for timely S phase entry in cells returning to proliferation from quiescence.

Our model (Fig. 1a,b) predicted an earlier and more rapid increase in CDK2 activity upon WEE1 inhibition. To test this, we used an exogenously expressed fluorescent live-cell reporter (CDK2L–GFP) that measures cyclin E–CDK2 activity in real time during G1 (ref. 17) (Fig. 2a). hTert-RPE1 mRuby–PCNA CDK2L–GFP cells were released from CDK4/6i G0/G1 arrest in the presence or absence of WEE1i, and CDK2 activity was tracked by time-lapse imaging. We used a WEE1i to perturb WEE1, as it was a clinically relevant drug, and because a higher fraction of cells entered S phase with this approach compared with siRNA or degron approaches, allowing us to make statistically meaningful comparisons. When WEE1 was inhibited, CDK2 activity started to increase earlier during G1 and reached a higher activity than in dimethyl sulfoxide (DMSO)-treated cells (Fig. 2b and Supplementary Video 1). Cells treated with WEE1i therefore entered S phase at a higher level of CDK2 activity. The observation that cells could remain in G1 with high CDK2 activity was surprising; we repeated the experiment using a similar, shorter version of the CDK2 sensor (DHB–mCherry) and observed the same result18 (Extended Data Fig. 3a,b).

a, Graphic of CDK2 activity sensor. b, CDK2 activity plotted relative to G1/S for cells released from G0/G1 arrest in the presence of DMSO (black) or WEE1i (red). Mean and 95% confidence interval from 1 of n = 2 biological repeats. a.u., arbitrary units. c, Timing and rate of APC/CCDH1 inactivation plotted relative to G1/S for cells released from G0/G1 arrest in the presence of DMSO (black) or WEE1i (red). G1/S was defined by mTurquoise2–PCNA. Change in mean over time is shown. Mean and 95% confidence interval of n = 2 biological repeats. Extra sum-of-squares F test comparing Hill functions fitted to the two curves (DMSO versus WEE1i) determined no significant difference. d, CDK2 and APC/CCDH1 activity plotted together for comparison of behaviour around G1/S (individual experiments in b and c). Data were in silico-aligned to G1/S, on the basis of fluorescent PCNA in each cell line. Top, DMSO-treated cells; bottom, WEE1i-treated cells. e, Top, protein levels of nuclear CDC6–mVenus plotted relative to G1/S for cells released from G0/G1 arrest in the presence of DMSO (black) or WEE1i (red). Mean and 95% confidence interval from 1 of n = 3 biological repeats. Bottom, measurements of the licensing window, defined by nuclear CDC6–mVenus protein accumulation (left), and peak nuclear CDC6–mVenus intensity (right). Mean ± s.d. are plotted in black, individual data points are coloured by experimental repeat. Significance was determined by unpaired Student’s t-test; ****P < 0.0001. CDC6–mVenus is rapidly exported to the cytoplasm at the end of G1 (ref. 25), hence the rapid drop in nuclear intensity. f, Quantification of chromatin-bound MCM7 at time points after release from CDK4/6i-mediated G0/G1 arrest in the presence of DMSO (grey) or WEE1i (red). Mean ± s.d. from one of n = 2 biological repeats. Significance was determined by one-way analysis of variance (ANOVA) followed by Šídák’s multiple comparisons test; ****P < 0.0001; NS, not significant.

Two key events drive the G1/S transition: an increase in CDK2 activity and inhibition of APC/CCDH1 E3 ubiquitin ligase activity19 (Fig. 1b). CDK2 can phosphorylate CDH1 and inhibit APC/CCDH1 activity20,21,22. Therefore, we tested whether there was any change in the timing and/or rate of APC/CCDH1 inactivation relative to the G1/S transition in WEE1i-treated cells that had abnormally high CDK2 activity in G1. We used hTert-RPE1 cells expressing mCherry–geminin(1–110), constitutively expressed from an exogenous promoter, as a live-cell reporter of APC/CCDH1 activity21. Geminin is very effectively degraded by APC/CCDH1 during G1, and geminin protein only starts to accumulate after the G1/S transition (Extended Data Fig. 3c). Therefore, mCherry–geminin, together with coexpressed mTurquoise2–PCNA (under the control of an exogenous promoter), is a useful reporter for determining the timing and rate of APC/CCDH1 inactivation relative to the G1/S transition. Using this reporter, we identified that in cells released from CDK4/6i G0/G1 arrest, APC/CCDH1 activity was switched off just after the G1/S transition in WEE1i-treated cells, with timing and rate indistinguishable from those observed in DMSO-treated cells (Fig. 2c and Extended Data Fig. 3c). Our data suggest that upon WEE1 inhibition, CDK2 activity increases to a higher level in G1, but the timing of APC/CCDH1 inactivation relative to the G1/S transition remains similar to that in DMSO-treated cells (Fig. 2d). Consequently, APC/CCDH1 inactivation is more important than reaching a threshold of CDK2 activity with respect to establishing the timing of S phase entry.

Low CDK2 activity during G1 provides a period for licensing of DNA replication origins23. An early increase in CDK2 activity in G1 in WEE1i-treated cells could decrease DNA replication origin licensing. CDC6 is an essential replication origin licensing factor that is required for formation of prereplicative complexes at replication origins24. We used an hTert-RPE1 cell line expressing mTurquoise2–PCNA, DHB–mCherry and CDC6–mVenus25, all under the control of exogenous constitutively active promoters, to track the dynamics of nuclear CDC6 protein relative to the G1/S transition. In cells released from CDK4/6i G0/G1 arrest in the presence of WEE1i, the licensing window (the time from the rise to the peak in nuclear CDC6–mVenus intensity25) was reduced compared with that in DMSO-treated cells (Fig. 2e). We also quantified replication origin licensing during G1 by quantifying chromatin-bound MCM protein in cells released from CDK4/6i G0/G1 arrest. We observed a decrease in chromatin-bound MCM7 protein in WEE1i-treated G1 cells compared with DMSO-treated cells (Fig. 2f).

In summary, these results demonstrate that WEE1 inhibits CDK2 activity during G1, and that an early and rapid increase in CDK2 activity after WEE1 inhibition can lead to reduced DNA replication origin licensing but not inhibition of APC/CCDH1 activity. Increased CDK2 activity is insufficient to accelerate the transition of WEE1i-treated G1 cells into S phase, suggesting that WEE1 has other roles during G1.

As progression through G1 was abnormal in WEE1i-treated cells, we examined whether such cells entered S phase normally. We observed that mRuby–PCNA localization at the start of S phase was abnormal in WEE1i-treated cells, with low PCNA intensity and less well-defined PCNA foci compared with DMSO-treated cells (Fig. 3a). In WT cells, as S phase progresses, mRuby–PCNA localization changes, with early DNA replication factories (small puncta) coalescing into larger DNA replication factories (large foci). Thus, the standard deviation of mRuby–PCNA intensity normally increases throughout S phase. We quantified the standard deviation of mRuby–PCNA nuclear intensity; this showed a clear reduction at the G1/S transition in WEE1i-treated cells compared with DMSO-treated cells (Fig. 3b). This decrease also remained throughout S phase, indicating altered S phase progression in the presence of WEE1i, consistent with previous reports26,27,28.

a, Images of mRuby–PCNA from DMSO-treated (top) and WEE1i-treated (bottom) cells. b, Quantification of the standard deviation of mRuby–PCNA intensity (Methods), relative to G1/S for cells released from G0/G1 arrest into DMSO (black) or WEE1i (red). Mean and 95% confidence interval for 1 of n > 3 biological repeats are shown. c, Quantification of PIP-mVenus intensity over time, plotted relative to G1/S for cells released from G0/G1 arrest into DMSO (black) or WEE1i (red). Mean and 95% confidence interval for 1 of n = 2 biological repeats are shown. One-phase exponential decay is denoted by dotted lines, and the half-life for each condition is shown. d, Quantification of p21–GFP nuclear intensity over time, plotted relative to G1/S for cells released from G0/G1 arrest in the presence of DMSO (control, black line) or WEE1i (red line). Mean and 95% confidence interval for 1 of n = 2 biological repeats are shown. e, Quantification of p21–GFP nuclear intensity over time, plotted relative to G1/S for cells released from G0/G1 arrest in the presence of WEE1i (red) or combined WEE1i and CDK1i (yellow). Mean and 95% confidence interval for 1 of n = 2 biological repeats are shown. f, Quantification of nuclear γH2Ax intensity in EdU-positive cells released from G0/G1 arrest for 48 h in the presence of DMSO or WEE1i. Significance was determined by Student’s t-test; ****P < 0.0001. Data from 1 of n = 2 biological repeats are shown. g, Cell cycle phase timings for p21-WT and p21-knockout (KO) cells treated with DMSO (left) or WEE1i (right) after release from CDK4/6i. Data from one of n = 2 biological repeats are shown. h, Quantification of G1 or G2 arrest in p21-WT and p21-KO cells treated with DMSO or WEE1i. Mean ± s.d. of n = 2 biological repeats are shown. Significance was determined by one-way ANOVA; *P < 0.05, ***P < 0.001. Scale bars, 20 μm.

PCNA recruitment to chromatin in S phase brings substrates, including DNA licensing factor CDT1 and CDK inhibitor protein p21, in contact with the CRL4CDT2 E3 ubiquitin ligase. This occurs through substrate binding to PCNA through a PCNA interaction protein (PIP) motif29, which leads to rapid substrate degradation and S phase progression. We assayed whether the change in PCNA chromatin recruitment in WEE1i-treated cells affected PIP-containing substrate degradation using an hTert-RPE1 cell line expressing the PIP-FUCCI reporter from an exogenous promoter and time-lapse imaging. We tracked the dynamics of the degradation of this construct, which contains the PIP motif of CDT1 fused to an mVenus fluorophore, at the G1/S transition. In WEE1i-treated cells, the degradation of PIP-mVenus was slower compared with that in DMSO-treated cells (Fig. 3c), suggesting that impaired recruitment of PCNA to chromatin at the start of S phase affects the rate of degradation of PIP-containing substrates.

p21 contains a PIP motif; therefore, p21 protein is normally rapidly degraded at S phase entry30,31,32, remains low throughout S phase and reaccumulates at the S/G2 transition when CRL4CDT2 is inhibited by CDK1 (ref. 33). As PIP-mVenus was more slowly degraded at the G1/S transition after WEE1 inhibition, we proposed that normal p21 protein degradation might also be impaired. To test this hypothesis, we used an hTert-RPE1 mRuby–PCNA cell line expressing endogenously labelled p21–GFP34. We observed that in WEE1i-treated cells, p21–GFP protein accumulated abnormally during S phase (Fig. 3d, Extended Data Fig. 4a,b and Supplementary Video 2). This suggested continuing impaired p21 degradation in S phase, not just at the G1/S transition, upon WEE1 inhibition. We further proposed that WEE1 inhibition in S phase would lead to increased CDK1 activity and premature inactivation of CRL4CDT2 (ref. 33). We tested whether treating cells with a CDK1 inhibitor would restore CRL4CDT2 activity during S phase and prevent accumulation of p21. Combining WEE1 and CDK1 inhibition prevented accumulation of p21–GFP in S phase cells treated with WEE1i alone (Fig. 3e and Extended Data Fig. 4c,d).

If p21 is not degraded during S phase, it can cause severe disruption to DNA replication and DNA damage35. Cells treated with WEE1i had increased levels of DNA damage compared with DMSO-treated cells (Fig. 3f and Extended Data Fig. 4e). These results indicate that abnormal p21 protein accumulation may contribute to DNA damage in WEE1i-treated cells.

High levels of p21 protein can cause cell cycle arrest in G2. We tracked the cell cycle fate of hTert-RPE1 mRuby–PCNA cells released from CDK4/6i G0/G1 arrest and treated with WEE1i. We observed an increase in the fraction of cells arresting in G2 in WEE1i-treated cells compared with DMSO-treated cells and determined that this was p21-dependent (Fig. 3g,h), whereas the increase in G1 arrest after WEE1i treatment was not p21-dependent (Fig. 3g,h). WEE1i-treated cells do not completely replicate their DNA during S phase; therefore, more accurately, this was a ‘G2-like’ arrest36. We confirmed that this was the case by examining cellular DNA content: we observed a large accumulation of cells between 2n and 4n specifically in WEE1i-treated cells (Extended Data Fig. 4f,g).

These data show that WEE1 inhibition leads to an abnormal G1/S transition that is exacerbated by the detrimental effects of WEE1 on DNA replication.

We next aimed to determine the mechanism preventing WEE1-inhibited G1 cells with high CDK2 activity from entering S phase prematurely. As APC/CCDH1 seemed to be inactivated with normal rate and timing around the G1/S transition in WEE1i-treated cells, we considered whether WEE1 inhibition could cause increased or prolonged APC/CCDH1 activity during G1 that could lead to a delay in S phase entry. This hypothesis was consistent with a previous report showing that WEE1 physically interacts with and can inhibit the ubiquitin ligase activity of APC/CCDH1 (ref. 37).

First, we revised our ordinary differential equation model to test the impact of WEE1 inhibition of APC/CCDH1 on the G1/S regulatory network (Fig. 4a). We considered three hypotheses for this interaction: first, that WEE1 inactivates CDH1 through a mechanism identical to that of CDK2 (‘CDK2-like model’), that is, WEE1 phosphorylates the same sites in CDH1 as in CDK2 (Extended Data Fig. 5a,b); second, that WEE1 does not inactivate CDH1 directly but rather increases the affinity of cyclin E–CDK2 for APC/CCDH1 (‘threshold model’), which has otherwise been shown to be negligible in vitro20,38 (Extended Data Fig. 5e,f); and, third, that WEE1 inactivates CDH1 independently of CDK2, by phosphorylating separate amino acid residues in CDH1 (‘independent model’) (Extended Data Fig. 5i,j).

a, Revised influence diagram for the G1/S network. b, Simulation from the revised ‘independent’ model. G1/S (grey) is determined by APC/CCDH1 switch-off and cyclin A–CDK2 increase. c, Quantification of CDC25A–GFP nuclear intensity over time, plotted relative to G1/S for cells released from G0/G1 arrest into DMSO (black) or WEE1i (red). Mean and 95% confidence interval for 1 of n = 2 biological repeats. Right, quantification of peak CDC25A–GFP intensity. Mean ± s.d., n = 2 biological repeats. Significance was determined by unpaired Student’s t-test; ***P < 0.001. d, G1 length in cells released from G0/G1 arrest under the conditions labelled. Mean ± s.d., n = 3 biological repeats. Significance was determined by one-way ANOVA followed by Tukey’s multiple comparisons test; ****P < 0.0001. e, Quantification of G0/G1 arrest in cells released from G0/G1 arrest under the conditions labelled. Mean ± s.d., n = 3 biological repeats. Significance was determined by one-way ANOVA followed by Tukey’s multiple comparisons test; ****P < 0.0001. In c–e, individual data points are coloured by experimental repeat. f, Immunoblot of in vitro kinase assay for WEE1 and CDH1. One of n = 2 biological repeats. g, APC/CCDH1 structure (PDB9GAW); the hydrogen bond between CDH1 Tyr91 and APC1 His1231 is highlighted. h, APC/CCDH1 structure; the intramolecular hydrogen bonds between CDH1 Tyr130 and Asp46 and Arg47 are highlighted. i, Cartoon showing how WEE1 phosphorylation of CDH1 could affect CDH1 binding and APC/CCDH1 activity. j, Left, CDH1 binding assay with WT and phosphomimetic CDH1. Endogenous APC/C was immunoprecipitated from Xenopus egg extracts. Right, quantification of CDH1 immunoblot, normalized to APC5 then to APC/C-bound WT CDH1. Mean ± s.d., n = 3 biological repeats. k, Left, APC/CCDH1-dependent destruction assay. Right, quantification of cyclin B levels over time, normalized to cyclin B Δ67, then to 0 min. Mean ± s.d., n = 3 biological repeats. The Thr129Asp CDH1 mutation is included as this site has been shown to be phosphorylated by mTOR43. NTC, non-targeting control.

All three models reproduced G0 arrest upon complete WEE1 depletion (Fig. 1d,e and Extended Data Fig. 5d,h,l). However, they differed markedly with respect to the emergent APC/CCDH1 activity during G1 and at G1/S upon partial (50%) WEE1 inhibition. Thus, each set of assumptions made distinct, experimentally verifiable predictions. The threshold model predicted a gradual inactivation of APC/CCDH1 around the G1/S transition (Extended Data Fig. 5f), which was inconsistent with our time-lapse data (Fig. 2c). This left the CDK2-like and independent models of CDH1 inhibition by WEE1. These two models differed principally in terms of the change in APC/CCDH1 activity during G1 after WEE1 inhibition (Extended Data Fig. 5a,b,i,j). In the CDK2-like model, APC/CCDH1 activity remained unchanged after WEE1 inhibition, although it was prolonged, whereas in the independent model, APC/CCDH1 activity increased after WEE1 inhibition. To differentiate between the two possibilities, we quantified levels of APC/CCDH1 activity during G1 in WEE1i-treated cells.

To estimate APC/CCDH1 activity in G1 cells, we used time-lapse imaging to quantify the levels of two APC/CCDH1 substrates that accumulate during G1: CDC6 (ref. 39) and CDC25A (ref. 40). We considered using the mCherry–geminin reporter, but as geminin is very efficiently degraded by APC/CCDH1, the mCherry–geminin signal intensity was negligible in G1 (Extended Data Fig. 3c). Quantification of the peak intensity of nuclear CDC6–mVenus protein from our previous analysis showed that CDC6–mVenus protein levels were decreased in WEE1i-treated G1 cells compared with DMSO-treated cells (Fig. 2e). This was owing to decreased CDC6–mVenus protein stability in WEE1i-treated cells (Extended Data Fig. 3d,e). To analyse CDC25A protein levels in real time, we generated an hTert-RPE1 mRuby–PCNA cell line in which we introduced a GFP fluorophore into the carboxyl terminus of the endogenous CDC25A gene (Extended Data Fig. 6a). We validated these cells (Extended Data Fig. 6b–f). In unperturbed cells released from CDK4/6i G0/G1 arrest, CDC25A–GFP showed accumulation around the G1/S transition (Fig. 4c and Supplementary Video 3). We confirmed that CDC25A–GFP was a reliable readout of APC/CCDH1 activity in G1 by depleting CDH1 using siRNA, then quantifying CDC25A–GFP levels and showing that they were increased (Extended Data Fig. 6g). After WEE1i treatment in cells released from CDK4/6i G0/G1 arrest, CDC25A–GFP levels were much reduced, compared with those in DMSO-treated cells, around the G1/S transition (Fig. 4c). This reduction was due to a change in CDC25A protein stability, rather than a change in CDC25A transcription (Extended Data Fig. 6h,i). The decreased CDC6 and CDC25A protein levels after WEE1i treatment during G1 were consistent with increased APC/CCDH1 activity after WEE1 inhibition and thus the independent model of WEE1 inhibition of CDH1 (Fig. 4b and Extended Data Fig. 5i–l).

To test whether increased APC/CCDH1 activity caused a longer G1 phase in WEE1i-treated cells, we used siRNA to deplete CDH1. This led to a short G1 length (Fig. 4d and Extended Data Fig. 7a). CDH1 depletion and simultaneous inhibition of WEE1 also led to a short G1 length, consistent with our model and supporting the hypothesis that in WEE1i-treated cells, APC/CCDH1 causes a prolonged G1 phase (Fig. 4d). Using an hTert-RPE1 cell line expressing exogenous, doxycycline-inducible EMI1 to inhibit APC/CCDH1 (ref. 41), we observed a robust decrease in CDH1 protein levels and a decrease in the proportion of G0/G1-arrested cells in the presence of WEE1i (Fig. 4e and Extended Data Fig. 7b). Notably, we did not observe any increase in CDH1 protein levels or change in CDH1 stability in G1 cells after WEE1i treatment (Extended Data Fig. 7c–e), suggesting that WEE1 does not regulate CDH1 protein stability.

To test whether WEE1 phosphorylated CDH1 directly, we used in vitro kinase reactions with purified WEE1 and CDH1 proteins. WEE1 phosphorylated tyrosine(s) in CDH1 (Fig. 4f and Extended Data Fig. 7f) in a time-dependent manner, whereas phosphorylation of related APC/C coactivator CDC20 was negligible (Extended Data Fig. 7g). Mass spectrometry analysis showed that Tyr91 and Tyr130 of CDH1 were phosphorylated by WEE1. We re-analysed the samples using a targeted liquid chromatography–mass spectrometry approach, focusing acquisition on the identified phosphorylated peptides. This provided exceptionally high-quality fragmentation data, allowing unambiguous localization of the phosphorylation sites and demonstrating phosphorylation of Tyr91 and Tyr130 of CDH1 by WEE1 (Extended Data Fig. 7h,i). Tyr91Phe–Tyr130Phe CDH1 mutants had substantially reduced WEE1-driven pTyr phosphorylation, indicating that these sites contribute substantially to CDH1 phosphorylation, although WEE1 may phosphorylate other sites (Extended Data Fig. 7j). These sites, particularly Tyr130, are conserved across species (Extended Data Fig. 8), and both phosphorylation events have previously been detected in cells, although the kinase responsible was not identified42,43.

Scrutinizing the structure of human APC/CCDH1 (ref. 44), we found that the Tyr91 and Tyr130 sites were part of a CDH1 amino-terminal extension that interacted extensively with APC/C. Tyr91 sat at an interface between CDH1 and APC1 and formed a hydrogen bond with His1231 in APC1 (Fig. 4g). Tyr130 formed intramolecular hydrogen bonds with Asp46 and Arg47 and seemed to stabilize the C-box motif in CDH1 (ref. 45) (DRFIP), which has a crucial role in maintenance of the interaction with APC8b (ref. 46) (Fig. 4h). These observations suggest that phosphorylation of CDH1 at Tyr91 and Tyr130 could weaken the engagement of CDH1 with APC/C and thereby reduce APC/CCDH1 activity (Fig. 4i). To test this, we examined whether phosphomimetic substitution of the WEE1-targeted residues affected APC/C binding by CDH1. In APC/C binding assays in Xenopus extract, both CDH1 Tyr91Glu and CDH1 Tyr130Glu showed reduced association with APC/C, with Tyr130Glu consistently causing the stronger defect. Notably, the CDH1 Tyr91Glu–Tyr130Glu double mutant was almost completely defective in APC/C binding (Fig. 4j). Consistent with this binding defect, in vitro cyclin destruction assays showed that CDH1 Tyr130Glu was impaired in APC/C activation, whereas CDH1 Tyr91Glu caused only a modest defect. The double phosphomimetic mutant was severely compromised (Fig. 4k). These findings support a model in which WEE1-mediated phosphorylation of CDH1 inhibits APC/CCDH1 by weakening CDH1 engagement with APC/C.

On the basis of our data and modelling, we propose a mechanism of G1/S control whereby WEE1 phosphorylates and inhibits APC/CCDH1, independently of CDK2-mediated inhibition of CDH1, to control G1 length and promote robust S phase entry.

Having shown that WEE1 may suppress APC/CCDH1 activity and maintain low levels of p21 protein during S phase, we next investigated the implications of these findings for WEE1i treatment in the clinic. To determine whether WEE1 inhibition resulted in similar phenotypes in cancer cells to those observed in non-transformed hTert-RPE1 cells, we repeated experiments in TP53-wild-type (WT) KU1919 bladder cancer and MCF7 breast cancer cell lines engineered to express endogenously labelled mRuby–PCNA. These cell lines were chosen as they have a functional p53–p21 pathway and are sensitive to CDK4/6i, which enabled us to robustly arrest cells in G0/G1. G1 length increased in WEE1i-treated KU1919 and MCF7 cells, and cells of both lines underwent significant G1 and G2 arrest after treatment with WEE1i, similar to phenotypes of hTert-RPE1 cells (Fig. 5a,b and Extended Data Fig. 9a–f). G2 arrest was p21-dependent, and we observed increased cell death during G2 in WEE1i-treated cells compared with DMSO-treated cells in the absence of p21 in all cell lines tested (Figs. 3g and 5a,c and Extended Data Fig. 9a). These data indicate that cells lacking p21 may be more sensitive to WEE1 inhibition. CDKN1A (encoding p21) is rarely mutated in cancers, except in bladder cancer, for which between 10% and 15% of patients have predicted loss-of-function mutations of p21 (ref. 47). These mutations occur early and can be present in normal bladders48. Therefore, we investigated whether WEE1i treatment could be used to eradicate pretumorigenic CDKN1A mutant lesions to prevent a subset of bladder cancers. We generated p21-knockout cell lines in the non-transformed bladder urothelial cell line HBLAK and found that these cells were indeed more sensitive to WEE1i than their WT counterparts (Fig. 5d and Extended Data Fig. 9g).

a, Cell cycle phase timings for p21-WT and p21-KO KU1919 bladder cancer cells treated with DMSO (left) or WEE1i (right), after release from CDK4/6i. Data from 1 of n = 2 biological repeats are shown. b, Fractions of KU1919 cells arrested in G1 or G2 phase in p21-WT and p21-KO cells treated with DMSO or WEE1i. Mean ± s.d., n = 2 biological repeats. Significance was determined by one-way ANOVA; **P < 0.01, ***P < 0.001. c, Percentages of cells dying, quantified by time-lapse imaging, in p21-WT and p21-KO hTert-RPE1, MCF7 and KU1919 cells treated with DMSO or WEE1i. Mean ± s.d., n = 2 biological repeats. Multiple unpaired t-tests were performed for comparison: *P < 0.05, **P < 0.001. d, Dose–response curves showing sensitivity of p21-WT and p21-KO (two clones, C1 and C2) non-transformed HBLAK bladder cell lines to WEE1i treatment (extra sum-of-squares F test, p21 WT versus p21-KO: P < 0.0001). Half-maximal inhibitory concentration (IC50) values: WT 0.38 μM, p21-KO C1 0.18 μM, p21-KO C2 0.21 μM. Mean ± s.d., n = 3 biological repeats. e, Dose–response curves showing sensitivity of cells treated with non-targeting control (grey) or CDH1-targeting (blue) siRNA, 24 h before treatment with WEE1i (extra sum-of-squares F test; P < 0.0001). IC50 values: non-targeting control 0.2 μM, siCDH1 0.11 μM. Mean ± s.d., n = 3 biological repeats. f, Dose–response curves showing sensitivity of parental (sensitive) and WEE1i-resistant MCF7 cells to WEE1i treatment (extra sum-of-squares F test, parental versus WEE1i-resistant; P < 0.0001). IC50 values: sensitive 0.1 μM, resistant 0.49 μM. Mean ± s.d., n = 3 biological repeats. g, Genes significantly upregulated or downregulated (blue) in WEE1i-resistant cells compared with parental (sensitive) cells from RNA sequencing analysis. TP53, CDKN1A, ANAPC7 and ANAPC10 are highlighted. h, Schematic showing the relationships of acute and chronic WEE1 inhibition (WEE1i) with CDH1 activity and p21 levels.

We reasoned that depletion of CDH1 might also sensitize cells to WEE1i by allowing the cells to enter S phase and incur WEE1i-mediated DNA damage. Indeed, siRNA-mediated depletion of CDH1 increased sensitivity of hTert-RPE1 cells to WEE1i (Fig. 5e).

To identify changes that could drive acquired resistance to WEE1i, we generated a WEE1i-resistant MCF7 cell line (Fig. 5f). We profiled these cells by RNA sequencing and found that WEE1i-resistant cells showed upregulation of nucleotide biosynthetic pathways, consistent with previous work showing that nucleoside supplementation reduces double-strand breaks caused by increased replication initiation in WEE1i-treated cells26 (Extended Data Fig. 9h–j and Supplementary Data Table 1). WEE1i-resistant cells also showed downregulated TP53 and CDKN1A expression, as well as components of APC/CCDH1 (ANAPC7 and ANAPC10, encoding APC7 and APC10, respectively) (Fig. 5g). These observations were consistent with our mechanistic findings, as cells would need downregulation of the p53–p21 and APC/CCDH1 pathways to enable continued proliferation in the presence of WEE1i. However, loss of these proteins would lead to increased sensitivity to acute WEE1i treatment, as we observed for p21 and CDH1 (Fig. 5c,e,h). Analysis of DepMap for genes that synergized with WEE1 loss identified several APC/CCDH1 components (Extended Data Fig. 9k).

In conclusion, our data suggest that CDKN1A mutation status or APC/CCDH1 subunit expression could be used to improve patient selection and potentially reduce toxicity in WEE1i clinical trials.

The substantial side effects associated with WEE1i treatment reported in clinical trials, including myelosuppression, gastrointestinal issues and fatigue, have been attributed to potential off-target effects on PLK1. An alternative explanation is that WEE1 has other functions. We report a role for WEE1 in the regulation of re-entry into the cell cycle from quiescence. This could account for some of the effects of WEE1i observed in healthy tissues.

Our findings lead to an apparent conundrum, in that WEE1 inhibits CDK2 to prevent S phase entry yet also inhibits APC/CCDH1 to promote S phase entry. We propose that WEE1 overall promotes S phase entry. By inhibiting APC/CCDH1 activity, WEE1 permits CDC25A protein to accumulate; this in turn reverses WEE1 inhibitory phosphorylation of CDK2. WEE1 may be required for initial inhibition of APC/CCDH1 before CDK2-mediated CDH1 phosphorylation and degradation. Future work involving mutation of WEE1 and CDK phospho-sites in CDH1 and identification of tyrosine phosphatases that act on CDH1 could determine the relative importance of each pathway with respect to S phase entry. mTOR-mediated phosphorylation of CDH1 Thr129 transiently inactivates APC/CCDH1 (ref. 43). This site is adjacent to CDH1 Tyr130, which is phosphorylated by WEE1, and phosphorylation of Ser131 has also been detected in cells42; these findings indicate a potential key role for this motif in regulation of APC/CCDH1 activity, probably involving stabilization of the interaction of the C-box in CDH1 with APC8. mTOR inhibition decreased Thr129 and Tyr130 phosphorylation in cells; however, mTOR only phosphorylates Thr129 (ref. 43). Therefore, mTOR phosphorylation may precede WEE1 phosphorylation of CDH1 Tyr130. Together, these data indicate that APC/CCDH1 may function as a signal integrator in G1 to determine whether cells commit to cell proliferation.

Our experimental data are most consistent with the model in which WEE1 inhibition of APC/CCDH1 activity is independent of CDK2-mediated APC/CCDH1 inhibition. However, there remain two discrepancies.
